		<item>
		<title>Supercharge regulated workloads with Claude Code and Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/supercharge-regulated-workloads-with-claude-code-and-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Bradley Wyman]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 05 Oct 2026 17:25:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0cbe16ca98bef589de7fcf6ba5ec623095bdb76d</guid>

					<description>Anthropic Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 are available on Amazon Bedrock in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. Learn how to use them with Claude Code, Anthropic's agentic coding tool, for compliance-aligned, AI-assisted development on regulated and ITAR workloads.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Please note that the following post is intended for informational purposes only. The approach detailed below may not be suitable for all organizations or compliance programs. It is important to evaluate this potential solution against the compliance requirements of your organization and any applicable regulatory obligations you may have.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The availability of Anthropic Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions introduces an on-ramp for AI-assisted development for workloads with regulatory or compliance requirements, including International Traffic in Arms Regulations (ITAR). Claude Sonnet 5 holds FedRAMP Class D (formerly High) certification and DoD Impact Level 4 and 5 (IL4/IL5) authorization, and Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 hold FedRAMP Class D certification on Amazon Bedrock (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/services-in-scope/FedRAMP/amazon-bedrock-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;verify current model certification status&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explore how to use these models on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; in AWS GovCloud (US) with &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/a&gt;, Anthropic’s agentic coding tool. With this solution, you can run AI-assisted workflows that speed up everyday development tasks while maintaining compliance alignment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="amazon-bedrock-in-aws-govcloud-us"&gt;Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US)&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/govcloud-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS GovCloud (US) Regions&lt;/a&gt; are designed specifically for US customers with elevated &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/govcloud-us/latest/UserGuide/govcloud-compliance.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;compliance needs&lt;/a&gt;. You can deploy generative AI workloads to sensitive environments while maintaining compliance controls. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; is built with security-focused features, including:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/data-protection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Built-in data protection&lt;/a&gt; where customer content isn’t stored, logged, or used to train AWS models or shared with third parties.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;FedRAMP Class D (formerly High) certification and DoD Cloud Service Provider (CSP) SRG IL4/IL5 authorization pathways, supporting government agencies’ compliance requirements. See the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/services-in-scope/FedRAMP/amazon-bedrock-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock models compliance page&lt;/a&gt; for current model-level certification status.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Integration with existing security controls and compliance frameworks available in AWS GovCloud (US), maintaining the same high security standards as other AWS Regions while providing additional authorization pathways.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US) supports two endpoint surfaces, bedrock-runtime and bedrock-mantle, both powered by the same underlying Mantle inference engine with Zero Operator Access (ZOA) architecture. The &lt;strong&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/strong&gt; endpoint uses the AWS SDK (InvokeModel and Converse APIs) and supports Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, Agents, and invocation logging. This makes it the recommended choice for most new applications, particularly those requiring audit trails. The &lt;strong&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/strong&gt; endpoint supports the Anthropic Messages API natively and provides access to capabilities currently available only on that surface, such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/exploring-the-zero-operator-access-design-of-mantle/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;server-side tools, background inference, and Projects&lt;/a&gt;. Both endpoints are available for Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5, and Claude Sonnet 5, with bedrock-runtime available in both AWS GovCloud (US) Regions (US-West and US-East) and bedrock-mantle available in AWS GovCloud (US-West).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="claude-code"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://code.claude.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/a&gt; is Anthropic’s agentic coding tool that reads your codebase, edits files, runs commands, and integrates with your development tools. Powered by models such as Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 on Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US), it operates directly in your terminal, your preferred integrated development environments (&lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/ide-integrations" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IDEs&lt;/a&gt;) such as VS Code and JetBrains, and in the background with the &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/agent-sdk/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Agent SDK&lt;/a&gt;. Claude Code understands your entire codebase and can work across multiple files and tools to get things done. Claude Code can:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Write code and fix bugs spanning multiple files across your codebase.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Answer questions about your code’s architecture and logic.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Execute and fix tests, linting, and other commands.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Search through Git history, resolve merge conflicts, and create commits and pull requests.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Connect to external tools and data sources with the &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt;, including the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt;, Terraform, and Kubernetes.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Spawn &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/sub-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sub-agents&lt;/a&gt; that work on different parts of a task simultaneously.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Customize behavior with &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/memory" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CLAUDE.md&lt;/a&gt; memory files, &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;skills&lt;/a&gt; for repeatable workflows, and &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/hooks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hooks&lt;/a&gt; for pre/post-action automation.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Automate recurring tasks and integrate with continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) through &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/github-actions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub Actions&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/gitlab-ci-cd" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitLab CI/CD&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see Anthropic’s articles: &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/tutorials" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code tutorials&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/claude-code-best-practices" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code: Best practices for agentic coding&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview-try-claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock-in-aws-govcloud-us"&gt;Solution overview: Try Claude Code with Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US)&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This section provides step-by-step instructions for setting up and configuring Claude Code to work with Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US), including prerequisites, installation commands, environment configuration, and verification steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you get started, make sure that you have the following in place:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://signin.amazonaws-us-gov.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS GovCloud (US) account&lt;/a&gt; with access to Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Appropriate &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; roles and permissions for Amazon Bedrock. At minimum, your IAM policy should include:
  &lt;ul&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;For bedrock-runtime: &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;bedrock:ListInferenceProfiles&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:GetInferenceProfile&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;For bedrock-mantle (if using the Mantle endpoint): &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle:CreateInference&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle:GetProject&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle:ListProjects&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle:ListModels&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;Alternatively, attach the &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrockMantleInferenceAccess&lt;/code&gt; managed policy.&lt;/li&gt;
  &lt;/ul&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access-modify.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model access&lt;/a&gt; to Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5, and Claude Sonnet 5 enabled in your AWS GovCloud (US) account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt; configured with valid AWS session credentials using short-term API keys or AWS SSO login.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="set-up-claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock-in-aws-govcloud-us"&gt;Set up Claude Code with Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After configuring AWS CLI with your credentials, install Claude Code using one of the following methods:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;macOS, Linux, WSL:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.sh | bash&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Windows PowerShell:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-powershell"&gt;irm https://claude.ai/install.ps1 | iex&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Windows CMD:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-dosbat"&gt;curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.cmd -o install.cmd &amp;amp;&amp;amp; install.cmd &amp;amp;&amp;amp; del install.cmd&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Homebrew (macOS/Linux):&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;brew install --cask claude-code&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For additional installation methods, see &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/quickstart" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code installation docs&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="option-a-interactive-setup-wizard-recommended"&gt;Option A: Interactive setup wizard (recommended)&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to your project:
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd your-code-project&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Launch Claude Code:
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;claude&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run through the login wizard:
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;/login&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At the login prompt, select &lt;strong&gt;3rd-party platform&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt;. Follow the wizard prompts to choose your authentication method, region (us-gov-west-1), and pin your models. The wizard saves configuration to your settings file automatically.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you have previously configured Claude Code, run &lt;code&gt;/setup-bedrock&lt;/code&gt; to reopen the wizard and update your credentials, region, or model pins.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="option-b-manual-environment-variable-configuration"&gt;Option B: Manual environment variable configuration&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For scripted or enterprise deployments, set the following environment variables:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK=1
export AWS_REGION='us-gov-west-1'
export ANTHROPIC_MODEL='us-gov.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5-5'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To use Claude Opus 5.5 as the primary model instead:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export ANTHROPIC_MODEL='us-gov.anthropic.claude-opus-5-5'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To pin specific model versions for consistent team deployments:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL='us-gov.anthropic.claude-opus-5-5'
export ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_SONNET_MODEL='us-gov.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5-5'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Then navigate to your project and launch Claude Code:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd your-code-project
claude&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h4 id="option-c-using-the-bedrock-mantle-endpoint"&gt;Option C: Using the Bedrock Mantle endpoint&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Bedrock Mantle supports the Anthropic Messages API natively and is available in AWS GovCloud (US-West). To route Claude Code through Mantle:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export CLAUDE_CODE_USE_MANTLE=1
export AWS_REGION='us-gov-west-1'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When both bedrock-runtime and bedrock-mantle are needed in the same session:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK=1
export CLAUDE_CODE_USE_MANTLE=1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Note: Guardrails and invocation logging are available exclusively through the bedrock-runtime endpoint. For deployments requiring these compliance features, use the bedrock-runtime endpoint (Option A or B).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="verify-your-configuration"&gt;Verify your configuration&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Verify that Claude Code is running by checking for the &lt;strong&gt;Welcome to Claude Code!&lt;/strong&gt; message in your terminal. Run the &lt;code&gt;/status&lt;/code&gt; command to confirm your model and provider. The provider line should show Amazon Bedrock or Amazon Bedrock (Mantle) depending on your configuration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about configuring Claude Code for Amazon Bedrock, see &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/amazon-bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="considerations-when-deploying-claude-code-to-your-organization"&gt;Considerations when deploying Claude Code to your organization&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With Claude Code now generally available, the next step is deciding how to deploy it across your organization. Consider your foundational architecture for security, governance, and compliance:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/identity-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Identity Center&lt;/a&gt; to centrally govern identity and access to Claude Code. This verifies that only authorized developers have access. Additionally, using IAM Identity Center, developers can access resources with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/singlesignon/latest/userguide/howtogetcredentials.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;temporary, role-based credentials&lt;/a&gt;, alleviating the need for static access keys and helping enhance security. Prior to opening Claude Code, make sure that you configure AWS CLI to use an IAM Identity Center profile by using &lt;code&gt;aws configure sso --profile &amp;lt;PROFILE_NAME&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;. Then log in using the profile you created: &lt;code&gt;aws sso login --profile &amp;lt;PROFILE_NAME&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consider automated configuration of default environment variables.&lt;/strong&gt; This includes the environment variables outlined in this post, such as &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_MODEL&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_SONNET_MODEL&lt;/code&gt;. This will configure Claude Code to automatically connect to Amazon Bedrock, providing a consistent baseline for development across teams. Organizations can start by providing developers with self-service instructions, or use &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/settings" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;settings files&lt;/a&gt; to manage configuration centrally.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consider implementing&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/solutions/guidance/claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Guidance for Claude Code with Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; &lt;strong&gt;for large enterprise deployments.&lt;/strong&gt; The guidance helps organizations deploy Claude Code while maintaining strict control over AI resource access, connecting to existing identity infrastructure and providing observability for developer productivity and usage patterns.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/quotas.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service quotas&lt;/a&gt; &lt;strong&gt;and set appropriate tokens per minute (TPM) and requests per minute (RPM) based on the number of active developers.&lt;/strong&gt; Make sure that you have enough TPM and RPM quotas to support your team’s usage. For guidance, follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/costs" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;rate limit recommendations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pin model versions for consistent team deployments.&lt;/strong&gt; Without pinning, model aliases such as &lt;code&gt;sonnet&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;opus&lt;/code&gt; resolve to Claude Code’s built-in defaults, which may change between releases. Claude Code defaults to Claude Opus 5.5 as its primary model, so an unpinned deployment is billed at the Opus per-token rate. To keep teams on Claude Sonnet 5.5, set &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_MODEL&lt;/code&gt; to its full model ID. Use &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_SONNET_MODEL&lt;/code&gt; to control when your team moves to a new model. In AWS GovCloud (US), use the &lt;code&gt;us-gov.&lt;/code&gt; prefix for cross-region inference profile IDs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Implement cost monitoring and per-user token guardrails.&lt;/strong&gt; Claude Code sessions can be token-intensive, particularly with Claude Opus 5.5, which carries a higher per-token cost than Claude Sonnet 5.5. According to Anthropic, Claude Opus 5.5 completes tasks using fewer tokens than Claude Opus 5, at a lower price per token. Consider the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Implement &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/publicsector/implementing-per-user-token-guardrails-for-amazon-bedrock-in-government-agencies/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;per-user token guardrails&lt;/a&gt; to enforce daily token limits per developer, with alerting at 80% and 100% thresholds. This pattern uses Amazon CloudWatch invocation logging, AWS Lambda, and Amazon DynamoDB to track and enforce limits in near real time.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use prompt caching to reduce costs and improve response times. Both 5-minute and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/amazon-bedrock-one-hour-duration-prompt-caching/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;1-hour TTL&lt;/a&gt; options are available for supported models.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Consider defaulting teams to Claude Sonnet 5.5 (lower cost than Claude Opus 5.5) and reserving Claude Opus 5.5 for tasks requiring deeper reasoning or longer autonomous runs. For workloads that require IL4/IL5 authorization, default to Claude Sonnet 5.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;See the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/guidance-for-claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock/blob/main/assets/docs/MONITORING.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code Monitoring Implementation guide&lt;/a&gt; for additional observability patterns.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consider permissions, memory, and MCP servers for your organization.&lt;/strong&gt; Security teams can configure managed &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/security" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;permissions&lt;/a&gt; for what Claude Code is and is not allowed to do, which cannot be overwritten by local configuration. In addition, you can configure &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/memory" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CLAUDE.md memory files&lt;/a&gt; across projects to auto-add common workflows, style conventions, and coding standards to align with your organization’s preferences. Deploy your CLAUDE.md file into an enterprise directory so all developers inherit the same baseline. Use &lt;a href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/claude-code/hooks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hooks&lt;/a&gt; to enforce pre/post-action automation such as auto-formatting or lint checks.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choose the appropriate endpoint for your compliance requirements.&lt;/strong&gt; The bedrock-runtime endpoint supports Guardrails, invocation logging, and cross-region inference within both AWS GovCloud (US) Regions (US-West and US-East). The bedrock-mantle endpoint supports the Anthropic Messages API natively and is available in AWS GovCloud (US-West). Guardrails and invocation logging are exclusive to bedrock-runtime today, so for deployments requiring comprehensive audit trails and content filtering, bedrock-runtime is recommended.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conduct a thorough security assessment before deployment.&lt;/strong&gt; Evaluate Claude Code’s capabilities against your organization’s security policies. Amazon Bedrock secures the inference layer, but Claude Code runs on local developer machines and requires separate evaluation and risk management. Apply controls such as managed permissions, invocation logging, and per-user token limits, as you would for other third-party software running in your environment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The availability of Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 on Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US) provides organizations with a compliance-aligned path to AI-assisted development for regulated workloads. Claude Sonnet 5, with FedRAMP Class D (formerly High) certification and DoD IL4/IL5 authorization, offers the strongest compliance coverage for sensitive environments. Claude Sonnet 5.5, with FedRAMP Class D certification, is a step up from Claude Sonnet 5 on coding and knowledge work at a lower cost per task. Claude Opus 5.5, with FedRAMP Class D certification, brings deeper reasoning and longer autonomous task capabilities for workloads where that certification level is sufficient.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Combined with Claude Code, teams can adopt agentic coding workflows directly in their terminal, IDE, or CI/CD pipelines while data remains within AWS GovCloud (US) infrastructure. Whether you choose the bedrock-runtime endpoint for full Guardrails and logging support, or the bedrock-mantle endpoint for native Anthropic Messages API access, you can tailor the deployment to your organization’s compliance and operational requirements.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="for-more-information"&gt;For more information&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/govcloud-us/latest/UserGuide/govcloud-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock in AWS GovCloud (US)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/solutions/guidance/claude-code-with-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Guidance for Claude Code with Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/services-in-scope/FedRAMP/amazon-bedrock-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model FedRAMP certification and DoD authorization status&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/09/claude-opus-5-5-aws-govcloud/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Opus 5.5 in AWS GovCloud (US)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

		<item>
		<title>New agent skill: Amazon SageMaker optimized generative AI inference for your coding agent</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/new-agent-skill-amazon-sagemaker-optimized-generative-ai-inference-for-your-coding-agent/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mona Mona]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 05 Oct 2026 17:23:19 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">cf6f95ea7bf1aafa4505939aad0e550912dc69a2</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker optimized generative AI inference introduces the aws-ai-ml skill through the Agent Toolkit for AWS, giving coding agents like Kiro, Claude Code, and Codex deep expertise in inference optimization and benchmarking. Describe what you want, and your agent generates executable SageMaker Python SDK v3 code to benchmark, recommend, and compare deployments.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Engineers increasingly use coding assistance tools to accelerate their development workflows. Today, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-ai-now-supports-optimized-generative-ai-inference-recommendations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI optimized generative AI inference&lt;/a&gt; introduces the &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill, available through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt;. This skill gives coding agents like &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt;, Claude Code, and Codex deep expertise in inference optimization and benchmarking. Install the skill, and your existing agent can benchmark endpoints, recommend deployment configurations, compare performance runs, and generate executable SageMaker Python SDK v3 code on your behalf. The &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill is a toolkit that plugs into any coding agent that supports the Model Context Protocol (MCP), turning it into a SageMaker AI inference optimization expert.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through what the skill enables, how to set it up, and how it helps you move faster from model to production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-bridging-intent-and-infrastructure"&gt;The challenge: Bridging intent and infrastructure&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI supports serverful hosting across real-time, batch, and asynchronous modes. It offers on-demand and reserved capacity, heterogeneous instances, virtual private cloud (VPC) isolation, automatic scaling, and integration with every SageMaker AI training path. The surface area is broad and deep, but most engineers do not arrive knowing which instance family or serving container will best serve their needs. They arrive with a use case: a performance target they want to hit, a cost envelope they need to stay within, or a model they need to evaluate before committing to production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agentic experience for SageMaker AI optimized generative AI inference closes this gap. You tell the agent what you want to accomplish, and it produces executable SageMaker Python SDK v3 code that you can review, modify, and run in your own environment. The agent asks targeted clarifying questions, generates code grounded in real benchmarks and measured performance data, and adapts to your business constraints the way a solutions architect would.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Throughout, you stay in control. Every step is visible in real time and expressed as code you can read and question. Nothing happens behind an opaque UI.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You can install the &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt; on your local machine, or use it within an Amazon SageMaker Studio JupyterLab space. Either way, you can go from zero to a working conversation in 10 minutes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="option-a-use-with-any-coding-agent-kiro-claude-code-codex-or-any-mcp-compatible-agent"&gt;Option A: Use with any coding agent (Kiro, Claude Code, Codex, or any MCP-compatible agent)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Install the Agent Toolkit for AWS.&lt;/strong&gt; If you haven’t already, set up the Agent Toolkit. This requires AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) 2.35+ and &lt;a href="https://docs.astral.sh/uv/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;uv&lt;/a&gt; installed.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws configure agent-toolkit&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This auto-detects your agents, installs skills, and configures the AWS MCP Server. For agent-specific setup (plugin install commands, MCP config), see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS getting started guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Install the &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill.&lt;/strong&gt; Add the SageMaker AI optimized generative AI inference skill to your agent:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npx skills add aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/skills/aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3: Confirm and begin.&lt;/strong&gt; Open your coding agent’s chat panel and ask: “What skills are available?” You should see &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; listed. After you confirm, describe your intent in natural language. Your coding agent now has SageMaker AI inference optimization expertise built in.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prerequisites:&lt;/strong&gt; Your AWS credentials must have permissions to call SageMaker AI APIs (creating endpoints, running benchmark and recommendation jobs). The skill generates code that runs under your credentials. No additional AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) configuration is needed for the skill itself.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; For Kiro and Claude Code, agents can discover skills at runtime. They can search for and load skills on demand through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/the-aws-mcp-server-is-now-generally-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS MCP Server&lt;/a&gt;, without any local installation. Ask your agent: “Search for AWS skills related to databases.” Refer to the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/tree/main/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;readme&lt;/a&gt; for discovering skills at runtime.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="option-b-use-within-amazon-sagemaker-studio"&gt;Option B: Use within Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you prefer to work inside a managed JupyterLab environment, you can use the skill in Amazon SageMaker Studio with a pre-configured image.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Open Amazon SageMaker Studio.&lt;/strong&gt; Navigate to Amazon SageMaker Studio in your target AWS account and AWS Region. Select your Studio domain and launch the Studio IDE from your user profile.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Create a JupyterLab space.&lt;/strong&gt; From the Studio landing page, choose &lt;strong&gt;JupyterLab&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create JupyterLab space&lt;/strong&gt;. Name the space (for example, &lt;code&gt;my-inference-opt&lt;/code&gt;) and keep the sharing setting &lt;strong&gt;Private&lt;/strong&gt; (skills only sync on private spaces). Under the &lt;strong&gt;Image&lt;/strong&gt; menu, select the image that includes the SageMaker AI optimized generative AI inference skill. This image ships pre-configured with the &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; agent skill and all necessary dependencies. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Run space&lt;/strong&gt; and wait for it to boot (5–10 minutes the first time).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Use a fresh space. A reused space with a locally modified skill version may not pick up the pre-configured image.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3: Open JupyterLab and launch a terminal.&lt;/strong&gt; After the space boots, open JupyterLab and choose &lt;strong&gt;Terminal&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 4: Authorize your coding agent.&lt;/strong&gt; In the terminal, authenticate with your coding agent using your identity provider. For example, with Kiro:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kiro-cli login --license pro --identity-provider &amp;lt;your-IdP-start-URL&amp;gt; --region us-east-1 --use-device-flow&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 5: Confirm and begin.&lt;/strong&gt; Open your coding agent’s chat panel and ask: “What skills are available?” You should see &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; listed. Once confirmed, describe your intent in natural language.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Troubleshooting:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;If the agent reports no skills are available, verify that your space is set to &lt;strong&gt;Private&lt;/strong&gt;. You can also check from the terminal:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ls ~/.kiro/skills/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;If that directory is empty but &lt;code&gt;/etc/sagemaker/skills/&lt;/code&gt; contains the skill files, run &lt;code&gt;restart-jupyter-server&lt;/code&gt;, refresh the page, and retry.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-you-can-do"&gt;What you can do&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agentic experience covers the following capabilities across the inference optimization lifecycle. You don’t need to know which capability to invoke. Describe what you want, and your agent figures out the next step or asks clarifying questions if anything is unclear.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="benchmark-an-existing-endpoint"&gt;Benchmark an existing endpoint&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you already have a model deployed on a SageMaker AI endpoint, you can ask the agent to benchmark it. Tell the agent which endpoint you want to test, and it generates a Python notebook that runs a load test using the &lt;code&gt;Workload.synthetic()&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;start_benchmark()&lt;/code&gt; APIs from the SageMaker Python SDK.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before running any benchmark, the agent confirms that your endpoint is safe to load-test, because benchmarking drives real traffic to a live endpoint.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When the benchmark completes, you get a quantitative performance report with:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput&lt;/strong&gt;: requests per second, output tokens per second.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency&lt;/strong&gt;: p50, p99, time-to-first-token, inter-token latency.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concurrency&lt;/strong&gt;: number of simultaneous requests supported.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;These are measured values from real load on real infrastructure, not estimates. The agent also recommends improvement mechanisms (such as prefill decoding) to boost performance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example prompt:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;em&gt;“Benchmark my Llama endpoint on SageMaker AI.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="find-the-right-instance-type-for-your-model"&gt;Find the right instance type for your model&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you have a model and need to find the right instance type to deploy it on SageMaker AI, tell your agent. It doesn’t matter where your model lives or how you obtained it:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tuned or custom model in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/strong&gt;: You trained or downloaded a model and stored it in S3. Provide the S3 URI and your optimization goal. Example prompt: &lt;em&gt;“I want to find the cheapest instance type to deploy my fine-tuned model on SageMaker.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Publicly available foundation model from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: You want to deploy a foundation model (FM) from the JumpStart catalog then provide the model ID. Example prompt: &lt;em&gt;“Find the best instance for model huggingface-reasoning-qwen3-8b on SageMaker AI.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model on the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/models" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face Hub&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: You want to use a model hosted on Hugging Face. Provide the model name. For gated models (such as Llama variants), your agent surfaces the license terms and asks you to accept them and provide your Hugging Face token. Example prompt: &lt;em&gt;“I want to deploy a Llama model from Hugging Face Hub. What’s the cheapest option?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In every case, your agent generates code that evaluates your model against candidate instances and configurations, then presents ranked deployment options with concrete performance metrics: throughput, latency percentiles, time-to-first-token, and concurrency. You choose based on your cost and performance requirements.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="compare-benchmark-runs"&gt;Compare benchmark runs&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you ran multiple benchmarks (for example, before and after a configuration change, or across two instance types), you can ask the agent to compare them. Provide the two benchmark job names, and the agent generates a comparison that computes deltas across key metrics: throughput, latency percentiles, and time-to-first-token.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The results show percentage changes where positive means better, giving you a single, interpretable summary of whether your change improved performance, degraded it, or had no meaningful effect.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If one of the benchmark runs doesn’t exist, the agent offers to run it first before proceeding with the comparison.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example prompt:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;em&gt;“I have two benchmark runs and I want to compare them. Which one is faster?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="benchmark-results"&gt;Benchmark results&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This table compares two deployed models on the same benchmark workload (512/256 tokens, concurrency 4). The Δ% column shows how much faster Model B (Qwen3-8B) is than Model A (Qwen3-1.7B) on each metric. A positive value means Model B is better.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Qwen3-1.7B (Model A)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Qwen3-8B (Model B)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Δ%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Output token throughput&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;188.2 tokens/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;271.2 tokens/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+44.1% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Per-user throughput&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;47.5 tokens/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;69.4 tokens/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+45.9% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Request throughput&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;0.736 req/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1.08 req/s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+46.7% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Inter-token latency&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;20.9 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;14 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+33.0% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Request latency&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;5,382 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;3,658 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+32.0% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2705.png" alt="✅" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Time to first token&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;67.5 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;166.3 ms&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;−146.5% &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/274c.png" alt="❌" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The two models run on different hardware. Qwen3-8B uses a 4-GPU &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (4x A10G), while Qwen3-1.7B uses a single L4 GPU (&lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt;). The deltas reflect approximately 4x the compute, not just the models themselves.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The takeaway: Qwen3-8B delivers approximately 44–47 percent higher throughput and lower end-to-end latency, largely thanks to the additional GPU compute. Qwen3-1.7B wins only on time-to-first-token, the expected advantage of a smaller model on a single GPU.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="putting-it-all-together"&gt;Putting it all together&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You don’t need to memorize capability names or know which workflow to request. The following table shows how common requests map to outcomes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;You say&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you get&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;“I already deployed a model and want to know how fast it is.”&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Quantitative performance report: throughput, latency percentiles, concurrency metrics from real load.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;“I have a model in S3 and I don’t know what instance to deploy on.”&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Ranked deployment options with cost, throughput, and latency for each candidate configuration.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;“I want to deploy a JumpStart model and find the cheapest instance. I only have the model ID.”&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Ranked deployment options, no S3 staging required. Gated-model alternative surfaced if needed.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;“I ran a benchmark before and after a change. Which one is faster?”&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Percentage change across all metrics indicating improvement or regression.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;“I want to optimize a Llama model from Hugging Face Hub.”&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;License surfaced, model staged to S3, then standard recommendation output.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If your request spans multiple capabilities (for example, staging a Hugging Face model and then getting deployment recommendations), the agent chains them naturally within the same conversation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-to-expect-from-the-agent"&gt;What to expect from the agent&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Your agent, equipped with the &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill, follows a few behaviors that make the experience predictable and safe:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It asks for what it needs.&lt;/strong&gt; If information is missing (such as an endpoint name or S3 URI), the agent asks you to provide it rather than guessing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It confirms before impactful actions.&lt;/strong&gt; Before running a benchmark that drives real traffic to a live endpoint, the agent warns you about the impact and asks for explicit confirmation.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It tells you when something is out of scope.&lt;/strong&gt; If you ask for something the agent can’t do (such as deploying a model), it explains what it can offer instead, such as generating the deployment configuration that you need.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It generates SageMaker Python SDK v3 code.&lt;/strong&gt; Every output is executable code you can inspect, modify, and run in your own environment.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/realtime-endpoints-delete.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Delete SageMaker AI endpoints&lt;/a&gt; created during benchmarking or recommendations.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-updated-jl-user-guide-shutdown.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Stop or delete your JupyterLab space&lt;/a&gt; if you used SageMaker Studio.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/DeletingObjects.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Remove S3 objects&lt;/a&gt; stored by benchmark and recommendation jobs in your SageMaker AI default bucket.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;aws-ai-ml&lt;/code&gt; skill for Amazon SageMaker AI optimized generative AI inference turns your existing coding agent into a SageMaker AI inference optimization expert. Whether you need to benchmark a live endpoint, find the cheapest instance for your model, compare configurations, or stage a Hugging Face model for evaluation, you describe what you want and your agent delivers measurable results.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, install the skill through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt; and add it to the coding agent you already use, or launch a pre-configured JupyterLab space in Amazon SageMaker Studio. For more information, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/whatis.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Making Amazon Quick enterprise-ready: Automated, auditable cross-account resource promotion</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/making-amazon-quick-enterprise-ready-automated-auditable-cross-account-resource-promotion/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Keshav Ganesh]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 05 Oct 2026 15:56:28 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e22db81f9de569eb9635812b9194903d60b6defd</guid>

					<description>Promoting Amazon Quick resources (agents, action connectors, knowledge bases, flows, and spaces) from a development to a production AWS account has been a manual, error-prone chore. This post shows how to automate cross-account promotion with an idempotent, auditable MCP server on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick is Amazon’s agentic AI companion built for work. You build agents that reason over your data, call action connectors, and carry multi-step tasks to completion. Promoting those resources (chat agents, action connectors, knowledge bases, flows, and spaces) from a development to a production AWS account, the way you would any other application, has been a manual and error-prone chore. This post shows how to automate it with an idempotent, auditable Model Context Protocol (MCP) server on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The building blocks of an agentic solution are its &lt;strong&gt;agents, action connectors, knowledge bases, and spaces&lt;/strong&gt;: chat agents configured with custom instructions, connectors for Slack, Jira, and other integrations, knowledge bases grounded in your own documents, and the Space that binds them together. Teams assemble and iterate on them quickly in a development account.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Most enterprises run separate AWS accounts for development and production, sometimes with quality assurance (QA) in between. When agents, action connectors, and knowledge bases are validated in a development account, there is no native, one-click way to promote them into the next account. Teams rebuild each resource by hand: recreate each agent with the same instructions and starter prompts, re-attach each agent’s action connectors, re-grant resource permissions, and reprovision the Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket, bucket policy, and data source behind each knowledge base. The work is slow, difficult to audit, and easy to get subtly wrong, which undermines the governance story enterprises need.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="manage-amazon-quick-resources-through-an-api"&gt;Manage Amazon Quick resources through an API&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick resources are programmable. Spaces, agents, action connectors, knowledge bases, and flows are managed through the Amazon Quick API (part of the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight API surface&lt;/a&gt;), which provides the full resource lifecycle, create, read, update, delete, and list. Anything a business user configures in Amazon Quick, an agent with its instructions and starter prompts, a connector, a knowledge base, or a flow, we can inspect, recreate, update, and govern programmatically, permissions included.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This programmable surface is what makes governed promotion possible. Instead of recreating each resource by hand, we read a resource and its permissions through the API and reapply them in another account exactly as they were. The migrator in this post composes the create, read, update, and list operations into one repeatable workflow, and it never issues a delete against the target, so a run only adds or updates.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the Quick Resource Migrator, a sample MCP server hosted on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, that automates cross-account promotion of Amazon Quick resources in a single tool call. It’s resource-driven: you pick a resource type (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, or space) and select by id, by name, or all. It’s idempotent (safe to re-run), and it copies permissions faithfully by describing the source and replaying the same actions in the target. The full source code is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/tree/main/infrastructure/multi-tenant-assets-deployment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The migrator promotes a chosen set of resources in a single call. It is an upsert: a resource that does not yet exist in the target is created, and one that already exists is updated in place. Every update is protected by a versioned backup written to Amazon S3 before the change, so each resource keeps a full history you can review and roll back to. A read-only preview reports exactly what a run would create or update before you commit, and the migration itself runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, so it can be driven from Amazon Quick or any MCP-compatible client.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="what-gets-migrated"&gt;What gets migrated&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Selection model:&lt;/strong&gt; Pick a resource type (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, or space) and choose resources by id, by name, or all. Selection is resource-driven. Migrate a resource type directly, or migrate a space to bring its linked resources across with it.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chat agents:&lt;/strong&gt; Recreated with their custom instructions, identity, tone, starter prompts, and welcome message, with their action connectors re-attached (remapped to the target account). When a space is migrated, its agents are re-linked to it automatically.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action connectors:&lt;/strong&gt; Recreated with their configuration. Secret values are never read from the source. Connectors are created with placeholder credentials and re-authenticated in the target.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge bases:&lt;/strong&gt; The knowledge base is registered in the target account, its data source is recreated, and its permissions are copied. For S3-backed knowledge bases the migrator also provisions the target bucket and its bucket policy (a distinct output of the migration). The documents (S3 objects) themselves are not copied.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flows:&lt;/strong&gt; Recreated in the target account from their definition. Because flow IDs differ across accounts, flows are matched by name: a same-named target flow is updated, otherwise a new one is created. Flow permissions are copied.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Spaces:&lt;/strong&gt; Recreated in the target account and re-linked to their agents, connectors, and knowledge bases (with resource Amazon Resource Names (ARNs) remapped to the target). Migrate the linked resources first so the target ARNs resolve. Space permissions are copied.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="key-design-principles"&gt;Key design principles&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource-driven selection.&lt;/strong&gt; You migrate one resource type at a time (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, or space), selecting by id, by name, or all. Agents are recreated with their action connectors re-attached. Spaces are recreated and re-linked to their agents, connectors, and knowledge bases, with ARNs remapped to the target account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Permission fidelity.&lt;/strong&gt; Permissions aren’t hard-coded. The server calls the relevant &lt;code&gt;Describe*Permissions&lt;/code&gt; API on each source resource and replays the identical action list in the target, remapping principals to registered users in the target account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Idempotency.&lt;/strong&gt; Every resource is created-or-updated. The server describes the target first and decides whether to create or update, so re-running a migration converges on the same state instead of producing duplicates or failing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Least privilege and isolation.&lt;/strong&gt; The source role is read-only. The target role holds only the actions the migration needs. The runtime authenticates callers with a Cognito JSON Web Token (JWT) and can run in virtual private cloud (VPC) network mode.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Safe, reversible updates.&lt;/strong&gt; Before the migrator updates any existing target resource, it writes a versioned snapshot of that resource, and its dependencies, to a dedicated backup bucket. If that backup can’t be written, the update is aborted. Every created or updated resource is also snapshotted, and a restore tool can roll any resource back to an earlier version.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The solution uses a three-account model. A central runner account hosts the MCP server on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. The server assumes a read-only role in the source account and a read-write role in the target account using AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS), so no long-lived credentials are stored anywhere.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-1-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-1-8.png" alt="Three-account architecture: a runner account hosts the MCP server on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and assumes roles into the source and target accounts" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture of the Amazon Quick Resource Migrator MCP server&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="component-responsibilities"&gt;Component responsibilities&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Responsibility&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore runtime (runner account)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Hosts the MCP server (&lt;code&gt;server.py&lt;/code&gt;). Assumes roles into the source and target accounts and orchestrates the migration. Runs in VPC network mode with a Cognito JWT authorizer.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Cognito (runner account)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;User pool, resource server, and a machine-to-machine app client. Issues the JWT (client-credentials grant, scope &lt;code&gt;invoke&lt;/code&gt;) that callers present to AgentCore.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runner execution role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The AgentCore execution role: Amazon CloudWatch Logs, telemetry, and &lt;code&gt;sts:AssumeRole&lt;/code&gt; into the source and target roles.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Migrator role (source account)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Read-only Quick Sight describe/list permissions plus knowledge base read.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Migrator role (target account)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Read-write Quick Sight create/update permissions, knowledge base and S3 write, and &lt;code&gt;ListUsers&lt;/code&gt; for principal resolution.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Backup bucket (runner account)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Encrypted S3 bucket that stores versioned pre-update and post-migration snapshots of target resources. The runtime writes to it directly. Restore reads from it. Optional (disabled when unset).&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: Architectural components&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="migration-flow"&gt;Migration flow&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Resolve resources: from the resource type and selector (id, name, or all), resolve the concrete resource IDs in the source account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Describe the source: describe each selected resource to capture its configuration and permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Connectors: recreate each connector. The authentication config is sanitized to the create (write) model with placeholder secrets, then re-authenticated in the target. Copy permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Knowledge bases: create the target bucket (&lt;code&gt;knowledge-base-&amp;lt;env&amp;gt;-&amp;lt;account&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;), bucket policy, data source, and knowledge base, then copy knowledge base permissions. S3 objects are not copied.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Agents: recreate each agent with its action connectors attached (remapped to the target account), then copy agent permissions. Agent-to-space linkage is restored when the space itself is migrated (see the space step).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Flows: recreate each flow from its definition, matched by name (flow IDs differ across accounts): update a same-named target flow or create a new one, then copy flow permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Spaces: recreate each space and re-link its agents, connectors, and knowledge bases with ARNs remapped to the target account (migrate those resources first), then copy space permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Report: return a JSON report of the created or updated resources, buckets, backups, skipped permissions, and any errors.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="tools-exposed-by-the-mcp-server"&gt;Tools exposed by the MCP server&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The server exposes five tools, all defined in &lt;code&gt;server.py&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="preview_migration-read-only"&gt;preview_migration (read-only)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;preview_migration&lt;/code&gt; takes a source account ID, a resource type (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, space, or all), a selector (id, name, or all), and an AWS Region. It returns an inventory of the agents, action connectors, knowledge bases, and flows that would be migrated, with names and types, without making any change. Use it as a dry run to confirm scope and to support a change-management approval step before promotion. When you also pass a target account ID, the response adds a source-to-target mapping that marks each resource CREATE or UPDATE, so you can see exactly what a migration would change before running it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="migrate_resources-full-migration"&gt;migrate_resources (full migration)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;migrate_resources&lt;/code&gt; takes source and target account IDs, a resource type (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, or space), a selector (id, name, or all), a region, source and target environment names (used in the knowledge base bucket name), and the Quick Sight service role name. It performs the full create-or-update migration described above and returns a structured report of everything it created, updated, and granted, along with any errors. Because it’s idempotent, you can run it repeatedly, for example on every release, and it converges on the same target state.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="list_backups-read-only"&gt;list_backups (read-only)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;list_backups&lt;/code&gt; searches the backup catalog and lists the available versions for each asset. Backups are the pre-update snapshots the migrator writes to the backup bucket, one versioned object per update, so you can see the full history for any migrated resource.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="get_backup-read-only"&gt;get_backup (read-only)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;get_backup&lt;/code&gt; returns the full stored backup for a given asset and version (the latest by default), including the captured resource configuration and its dependencies.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="restore_backup"&gt;restore_backup&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;restore_backup&lt;/code&gt; re-applies a stored backup version onto the target resource, updating it in place, or recreating it if it no longer exists. It first takes a fresh pre-restore backup, so the revert is itself reversible.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="deploy-the-solution"&gt;Deploy the solution&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The full source and step-by-step deployment instructions live in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/tree/main/infrastructure/multi-tenant-assets-deployment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples repository&lt;/a&gt; README. At a high level you deploy three AWS CloudFormation stacks, the cross-account AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles, the VPC network, and the Cognito-authenticated AgentCore runtime that hosts the MCP server, then register that runtime as an action connector in Amazon Quick.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="use-the-migrator-through-a-quick-app"&gt;Use the migrator through a Quick App&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;By registering the runtime as an action connector, you can drive the migrator from Amazon Quick in natural language, and you can also build a Quick App: a point-and-click web experience layered on the same MCP tools. You don’t build that UI by hand. The repository includes a ready-to-use &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/blob/main/infrastructure/multi-tenant-assets-deployment/docs/quick-app-integration.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;app-builder prompt&lt;/a&gt; that you paste into the Amazon Quick app builder, replacing the placeholder connector and action IDs with your own to generate the app. The app turns the workflow into a guided flow: choose a source and target account and the resources to promote, preview what will be created or updated, run the migration, and review a history of every past migration, each backed by the versioned S3 snapshots the server writes. The following screens show that experience.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="an-example-quick-app"&gt;An example Quick App&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Because the app is generated from a prompt, each build differs. The following screens show one such example. In it, the landing page prompts for the Source Account ID, Target Account ID, resource type (agent, connector, knowledge base, flow, or space), and selector (id, name, or all), with additional options available as needed.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-2-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-2-8.png" alt="Quick App landing page with fields for source and target account IDs, resource type, and selector"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;Open the History tab to see every past migration. Each entry is backed by the versioned snapshot the migrator wrote to Amazon S3, so you get a full, auditable record of what was promoted and when, with the option to inspect any earlier version.&lt;/li&gt;

&lt;ol start="5" type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;To roll back, select a resource’s backup version and choose &lt;strong&gt;Restore&lt;/strong&gt;. The app re-applies that saved version to the target. Because it first captures a fresh backup before restoring, the revert is itself reversible.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/01/Figure-7.png" alt="Restoring a resource to an earlier backup version" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Rolling back a resource to an earlier backup version&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Cross-account promotion is a baseline expectation for enterprise software, and until now it has been the missing piece for Amazon Quick. The Quick Resource Migrator turns a slow, manual, hard-to-audit task into a fast, repeatable, and governed one: a single tool call recreates agents, connectors, and knowledge bases in a target account and copies permissions faithfully, all idempotently, so you can run it on every release.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-quick-suite-knowledge-hub/tree/main/infrastructure/multi-tenant-assets-deployment" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;, deploy it into a runner account, and try promoting an agent or knowledge base from development to production. Then adapt the pattern to your own governance and hardening requirements.&lt;/p&gt;
