			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Agentic retrieval with LangChain and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/agentic-retrieval-with-langchain-and-amazon-bedrock-knowledge-bases/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Manideep Reddy Gillela]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 05 Oct 2026 15:53:56 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9ef1f1b789cbe6fd45278d748b65ebf4500d6c5e</guid>

					<description>Build a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) application on Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base with LangChain, and see how agentic retrieval handles the multi-part questions that single-shot retrieval answers poorly. Run the same query through both paths, read the trace events, and compare what each retrieval path costs.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When a user asks the support assistant, a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) application built with LangChain to compare two products across three dimensions, they’re effectively posing six questions simultaneously. Similarity search uses a single query vector to encapsulate all the intents. The retriever then generates the best approximation of the average of those intents. The resulting answer comes back concise. The search executes without errors. The relevance scores look reasonable. Yet the retrieved chunks, while topically relevant, only cover a fraction of what the question actually asked.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showcase a RAG application on Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base with &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangChain&lt;/a&gt;. We run the same multi-part question through standard and agentic retrieval, and read the trace events to see the plan the model produced. We also cover what the two retrieval paths cost and when the cheaper one is the right choice.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Agentic retrieval is available on Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base. Instead of one search, Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base plans the retrieval. It breaks the question into sub-queries, runs them, judges whether it has enough evidence, and searches again if it doesn’t. The &lt;code&gt;langchain-aws&lt;/code&gt; package exposes both agentic and standard retrieval, so you can use either from a LangChain application.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-amazon-bedrock-managed-knowledge-base-for-faster-more-accurate-enterprise-ai-applications/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt;, the fully managed RAG capability in Amazon Bedrock, removes the self-managed vector store, embeddings, and re-ranking models from the RAG architecture. You configure a data source, and Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases handles chunking, embedding, storage, and retrieval. This walkthrough uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/serv-s3/?trk=50b671a1-06f5-4224-9505-fa45ee881c08&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=EAIaIQobChMI1uiUo4eXlgMVy1J_AB2pfydUEAAYASAAEgJ6PPD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23522747487&amp;amp;gads_ag=196433733807&amp;amp;gads_ad=795876995201&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20s3&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9022830&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23522747487&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp-DJgH1kF6NUslUNqoqqj14q&amp;amp;gclid=EAIaIQobChMI1uiUo4eXlgMVy1J_AB2pfydUEAAYASAAEgJ6PPD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases provides two APIs. We briefly discuss those differences in this post. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_agent-runtime_Retrieve.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Retrieve&lt;/a&gt; API runs one hybrid search and returns scored chunks. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_agent-runtime_AgenticRetrieveStream.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/a&gt; API runs a planning loop and streams the steps back to you as trace events. In the &lt;code&gt;langchain-aws&lt;/code&gt; package, the first is a standard LangChain retriever you can drop into a chain. The second is a function retrieval directly from a knowledge base.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the solution architecture. The application queries Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases using either the Retrieve API (standard, single-shot) or the AgenticRetrieveStream API (multi-step planning loop). Both paths return document chunks from the knowledge base, which the application then uses to generate a grounded response.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-21691-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-21691-1.png" alt="Application querying Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases through the Retrieve and AgenticRetrieveStream APIs to return document chunks and generate a grounded response" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture for querying Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases with the Retrieve and AgenticRetrieveStream APIs&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="implementation-walkthrough"&gt;Implementation walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following sections walk you through creating a knowledge base, querying it with both retrieval methods, and reading the trace events the agentic planner produces.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To follow along you need:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock in a Region where Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases and agentic retrieval are available. This walkthrough uses the US East (N. Virginia) Region (&lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;), and the code assumes it throughout. Check the AWS &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt; for other Regional availability and support.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Two AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) identities, described in the next section: a service role the knowledge base assumes, and permissions on the identity you call the APIs from.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.python.org/downloads/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Python 3.12&lt;/a&gt; or later.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An S3 bucket holding the sample documents. The corpus needs several documents that cover overlapping topics so that a comparative question has somewhere to go. A single flat document cannot demonstrate query planning.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Install the packages. The &lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Boto3&lt;/a&gt; version matters: &lt;code&gt;agentic_retrieve_stream&lt;/code&gt; did not exist before 1.43.32.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;langchain-aws&amp;gt;=1.6.3
langchain&amp;gt;=1.0
boto3&amp;gt;=1.43.32&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="permissions"&gt;Permissions&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Two identities are involved and separating them is worth doing deliberately. The knowledge base assumes a service role to read your documents and call the embedding model. Your application uses an AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS) caller identity to query. Neither needs the other’s permissions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock creates the service role for you if you let it. To supply your own, give it a trust policy that lets Amazon Bedrock assume it. Scope it with &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceAccount&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceArn&lt;/code&gt; so that another account can’t use it as a confused deputy:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
        "Effect": "Allow",
        "Principal": {"Service": "bedrock.amazonaws.com"},
        "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
        "Condition": {
            "StringEquals": {"aws:SourceAccount": "111122223333"},
            "ArnLike": {
                "aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:111122223333:knowledge-base/*"
            }
        }
    }]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The service role also needs &lt;code&gt;s3:ListBucket&lt;/code&gt; on your bucket and &lt;code&gt;s3:GetObject&lt;/code&gt; on its contents, both conditioned on &lt;code&gt;aws:ResourceAccount&lt;/code&gt;. Scope the &lt;code&gt;knowledge-base/*&lt;/code&gt; wildcard character down to specific knowledge base IDs after you have created them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The AWS STS caller identity needs a different set. &lt;code&gt;bedrock:AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; can’t be scoped to a knowledge base Amazon Resource Name (ARN). &lt;code&gt;bedrock:Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:GetDocumentContent&lt;/code&gt; can:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "AgenticRetrievalAndPlannerModel",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock:AgenticRetrieveStream",
                "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        },
        {
            "Sid": "RetrieveAndFullDocumentExpansion",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["bedrock:Retrieve", "bedrock:GetDocumentContent"],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;:111122223333:knowledge-base/&amp;lt;knowledge-base-id&amp;gt;"
        },
        {
            "Sid": "GenerateAnswersInTheChains",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModel", "bedrock:Converse", "bedrock:ConverseStream"],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;bedrock:GetDocumentContent&lt;/code&gt; is often overlooked. Agentic retrieval calls it when a &lt;code&gt;FullDocumentExpansion&lt;/code&gt; step decides a passage lacks the context to answer. A policy with only &lt;code&gt;bedrock:Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; works until the planner reaches for a whole document and then fails partway through a query.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To create and manage the knowledge base itself, the calling role additionally needs &lt;code&gt;bedrock:CreateKnowledgeBase&lt;/code&gt; on &lt;code&gt;*&lt;/code&gt;, and the &lt;code&gt;GetKnowledgeBase&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;UpdateKnowledgeBase&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;DeleteKnowledgeBase&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;StartIngestionJob&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;GetIngestionJob&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;ListIngestionJobs&lt;/code&gt; actions on &lt;code&gt;knowledge-base/*&lt;/code&gt;. If you’re using guardrails, add &lt;code&gt;bedrock:GetGuardrail&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:ApplyGuardrail&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Running this walkthrough might incur costs for document storage and ingestion in the knowledge base, retrieval calls, and foundation model (FM) inference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For more information about pricing, see the Knowledge Bases section of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Delete the resources when you complete this experiment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="creating-and-populating-the-knowledge-base"&gt;Creating and populating the knowledge base&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Create the knowledge base with a &lt;code&gt;managedKnowledgeBaseConfiguration&lt;/code&gt;. Setting &lt;code&gt;embeddingModelType&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;MANAGED&lt;/code&gt; uses the service-managed embedding model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3
import os

REGION = os.environ["AWS_REGION"]
bedrock_agent = boto3.client("bedrock-agent", region_name=REGION)

response = bedrock_agent.create_knowledge_base(
    name=KB_NAME,
    roleArn=KB_ROLE_ARN,
    knowledgeBaseConfiguration={
        "type": "MANAGED",
        "managedKnowledgeBaseConfiguration": {
            "embeddingModelType": "MANAGED",
        },
    },
)
KB_ID = response["knowledgeBase"]["knowledgeBaseId"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;There’s no &lt;code&gt;storageConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; in that request. For a self-managed knowledge base you would pass one describing your vector store. Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base does not take one, which is the clearest signal in the API that Amazon Bedrock owns the storage layer.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Attach the S3 bucket as a data source, then start an ingestion job. Ingestion is asynchronous, so poll until the job reaches a terminal state rather than sleeping for a fixed interval and hoping.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import time

SUCCESS_STATES = frozenset({"COMPLETE"})
FAILURE_STATES = frozenset({"FAILED", "STOPPED"})

def wait_for_ingestion(kb_id, ds_id, job_id, timeout_s=1800):
    """Poll an ingestion job until it reaches a terminal state."""
    deadline = time.time() + timeout_s
    while time.time() &amp;lt; deadline:
        job = bedrock_agent.get_ingestion_job(
            knowledgeBaseId=kb_id,
            dataSourceId=ds_id,
            ingestionJobId=job_id,
        )["ingestionJob"]
        status = job["status"]
        if status in SUCCESS_STATES:
            return job
        if status in FAILURE_STATES:
            reasons = job.get("failureReasons") or ["no reason reported"]
            raise RuntimeError(f"Ingestion job {job_id} finished as {status}: " + "; ".join(reasons))
        time.sleep(15)
    raise TimeoutError(f"Ingestion job {job_id} did not finish in {timeout_s}s")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The full data source configuration and error handling are in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-rag-bedrock-langchain-blog" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="querying-with-the-langchain-retriever"&gt;Querying with the LangChain retriever&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain-aws/retrievers/bedrock/AmazonKnowledgeBasesRetriever" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AmazonKnowledgeBasesRetriever&lt;/a&gt; wraps the Retrieve API and behaves like any other LangChain retriever. For Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases, pass &lt;code&gt;managedSearchConfiguration&lt;/code&gt;. This is the part that trips people up: &lt;code&gt;vectorSearchConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; is the previous path for knowledge bases where you run your own vector store. It is what most existing examples show.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from langchain_aws.retrievers import AmazonKnowledgeBasesRetriever

SIMPLE_QUERY = "What is the restore time objective for the checkout service?"

retriever = AmazonKnowledgeBasesRetriever(
    knowledge_base_id=KB_ID,
    region_name=REGION,
    retrieval_config={
        "managedSearchConfiguration": {
            "numberOfResults": 5,
        }
    },
)
docs = retriever.invoke(SIMPLE_QUERY)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each result comes back as a LangChain &lt;code&gt;Document&lt;/code&gt;. The relevance score is in &lt;code&gt;metadata["score"]&lt;/code&gt;, and the source document’s own metadata is under &lt;code&gt;metadata["source_metadata"]&lt;/code&gt;, renamed so it does not collide. If you want to drop low-confidence results, set &lt;code&gt;min_score_confidence&lt;/code&gt; on the retriever instead of filtering afterward.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For a question with one clear intent, this is the right tool. It is one call. The latency is the lowest of the two options, and you keep full control of how the answer gets generated. Most of the queries a production assistant sees are this shape, and reaching for a planning loop to answer them wastes money and time.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="where-single-shot-retrieval-runs-out"&gt;Where single-shot retrieval runs out&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Now give the same retriever a question with several parts:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;COMPLEX_QUERY = (
    "Compare the checkout and inventory services across on-call escalation, backup and "
    "restore targets, and deployment rollback procedure. Where do they differ?"
)

docs = retriever.invoke(COMPLEX_QUERY)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Five chunks come back, ranked by hybrid score against one embedding of that whole question.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That question contains six intents: two services across three dimensions. Scoring the retrieved text for evidence of each one gives a concrete measure of what a single embedding recovers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;numberOfResults&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chunks&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Share of corpus&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sub-intents covered&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Missing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;10%&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;4 of 6&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;checkout on-call, inventory restore&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;10&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;10&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;19%&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;6 of 6&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;none&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At five results, one embedding standing for six intents misses two of them. At ten it covers all six, with visible waste: two sub-intents are covered twice and one chunk carries none.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The retriever did its job. The limitation is structural: one vector cannot represent six intents, and there is no step in the process that asks whether the returned evidence is enough to answer the question.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="running-agentic-retrieval"&gt;Running agentic retrieval&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Agentic retrieval is not a LangChain retriever, but a feature of Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Bases. The &lt;code&gt;langchain-aws&lt;/code&gt; package exposes it as a standalone function, &lt;a href="https://python.langchain.com/api_reference/aws/retrievers/langchain_aws.retrievers.bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agentic_retrieve&lt;/a&gt;, because the underlying API streams its results and doesn’t fit the synchronous &lt;code&gt;BaseRetriever&lt;/code&gt; interface. There’s no flag on &lt;code&gt;AmazonKnowledgeBasesRetriever&lt;/code&gt; that switches it on.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from langchain_aws.retrievers.bedrock import agentic_retrieve

result = agentic_retrieve(
    knowledge_base_id=KB_ID,
    query=COMPLEX_QUERY,
    region_name=REGION,
    generate_response=True,
    number_of_results=10,
)
print(result["generatedResponse"]["answer"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;generate_response=True&lt;/code&gt;, the service returns a grounded answer and citations alongside the retrieved chunks, so you get an answer without wiring up a separate model call. The function works only against Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Internally, the service plans, retrieves, evaluates whether the evidence is sufficient, and iterates if it isn’t. The helper hides all of that and hands back the final chunks, which is convenient and means you can’t see the plan.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="reading-the-trace-events"&gt;Reading the trace events&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To watch the model decompose the question, call &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/boto3/latest/reference/services/bedrock-agent-runtime/client/agentic_retrieve_stream.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agentic_retrieve_stream&lt;/a&gt; on the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/boto3/latest/reference/services/bedrock-agent-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;bedrock-agent-runtime&lt;/a&gt; client directly. This is the one place in this walkthrough where we step around &lt;code&gt;langchain-aws&lt;/code&gt;, because the helper discards trace events and doesn’t expose &lt;code&gt;maxAgentIteration&lt;/code&gt; or a custom planner model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;runtime = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name=REGION)

response = runtime.agentic_retrieve_stream(
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": {"text": COMPLEX_QUERY}}],
    retrievers=[{
        "configuration": {
            "knowledgeBase": {
                "knowledgeBaseId": KB_ID,
                "retrievalOverrides": {"maxNumberOfResults": 10},
            }
        }
    }],
    agenticRetrieveConfiguration={
        "foundationModelType": "MANAGED",
        "rerankingModelType": "MANAGED",
        # 5 is the API default. Below 4 the planner stops decomposing entirely.
        "maxAgentIteration": 5,
    },
    generateResponse=False,
)

for event in response["stream"]:
    if "traceEvent" in event:
        attrs = event["traceEvent"]["attributes"]
        print(f"{attrs.get('step')}: {attrs.get('status')}")
        for action in attrs.get("actions", []) or []:
            if "retrieve" in action:
                query = action["retrieve"].get("inputQuery", {}).get("text", "")
                print(f" sub-query: {query}")
    elif "result" in event:
        for chunk in event["result"].get("results", []):
            print(chunk.get("content", {}).get("text", "")[:120])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;generateResponse&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;False&lt;/code&gt; when you only want the retrieval behavior. The API generates a grounded answer by default, which costs an extra model call you may not need while you are inspecting the plan.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A trace event’s &lt;code&gt;step&lt;/code&gt; tells you where the planner is. &lt;code&gt;SpeculativeRetrieval&lt;/code&gt; runs before the first plan to cut latency and doesn’t count against your iteration budget. &lt;code&gt;Planning&lt;/code&gt; is where the model reads the question and prior results and emits sub-queries. &lt;code&gt;Retrieval&lt;/code&gt; fires once per sub-query. &lt;code&gt;FullDocumentExpansion&lt;/code&gt; appears when the model decides a passage lacks the context to answer and pulls the whole document instead. Each carries a status of &lt;code&gt;IN_PROGRESS&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;SUCCEEDED&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;FAILED&lt;/code&gt;, plus a human-readable message.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The final chunks arrive separately. The &lt;code&gt;result&lt;/code&gt; is its own event type rather than a fifth step, and it holds the deduplicated chunks from every iteration along with the grounded answer when response generation is on. Branch on the event key, as the preceding loop demonstrates, rather than expect a terminal step value.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The sub-query text is the part worth logging. It sits in &lt;code&gt;attributes.actions[].retrieve.inputQuery.text&lt;/code&gt;, not in the top-level trace fields, so a handler that reads only &lt;code&gt;step&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;status&lt;/code&gt; shows you that planning happened without showing you what it decided.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the agentic retrieval planning loop, including the speculative retrieval, planning, sub-query retrieval, evaluation, and optional re-planning steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-21691-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-21691-2.png" alt="The agentic retrieval planning loop, from speculative retrieval through planning, sub-query retrieval, evaluation, and optional re-planning" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Steps in the agentic retrieval planning loop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Two details are worth knowing before you build on this. Deduplication applies only to the &lt;code&gt;result&lt;/code&gt; event, so a chunk retrieved by three sub-queries appears once at the end but three times across the traces.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The second is about scores. A Retrieve response gives each chunk a typed &lt;code&gt;score&lt;/code&gt; field holding its relevance to the query. Agentic retrieval results carry &lt;code&gt;content&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;metadata&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;sourceRetriever&lt;/code&gt;, with no equivalent typed field. Code that reads &lt;code&gt;result["score"]&lt;/code&gt; after switching APIs gets nothing. If you rank or filter relevance, plan for that difference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In production, use Amazon Bedrock Guardrails to enforce content policies and grounding checks on generated responses. Both retrieval paths support guardrails. Agentic retrieval supports guardrails through &lt;code&gt;policyConfiguration.bedrockGuardrailConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; rather than the &lt;code&gt;guardrail_config&lt;/code&gt; argument the LangChain retriever takes, and supports &lt;code&gt;BLOCK&lt;/code&gt; mode only. If you rely on &lt;code&gt;MASK&lt;/code&gt; mode, that is a reason to stay on the Retrieve API.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxAgentIteration&lt;/code&gt; accepts two through ten and defaults to five. Leave it at the default. At two or three the planner runs one cycle, emits no sub-queries, and returns what the speculative retrieval step already found. This is single-shot behavior at the agentic price. Decomposition begins at four. The planner often stops early when it judges the evidence sufficient, so the ceiling is a bound rather than a target.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="comparing-the-two-retrieval-paths"&gt;Comparing the two retrieval paths&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For context on how this behaves at scale, AWS evaluated agentic retrieval on MuSiQue, a public multi-hop benchmark. The evaluation showed improved recall over single-shot retrieval, with the largest gains on the hardest questions. Single-hop questions saw gains under five points. That last figure matches the shape of the trade-off: decomposition helps when there is something to decompose.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="building-the-rag-chain"&gt;Building the RAG chain&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For the standard retriever, the usual &lt;a href="https://python.langchain.com/docs/concepts/lcel/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangChain Expression Language (LCEL)&lt;/a&gt; composition works directly:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from langchain_aws import ChatBedrockConverse
from langchain_core.output_parsers import StrOutputParser
from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_core.runnables import RunnablePassthrough

def format_docs(docs):
    return "\n\n".join(doc.page_content for doc in docs)

llm = ChatBedrockConverse(model=MODEL_ID, region_name=REGION)
prompt = ChatPromptTemplate.from_template(PROMPT_TEMPLATE)

chain = (
    {"context": retriever | format_docs, "question": RunnablePassthrough()}
    | prompt
    | llm
    | StrOutputParser()
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;format_docs&lt;/code&gt; matters more than it looks. Passing &lt;code&gt;Document&lt;/code&gt; objects straight into a prompt renders their &lt;code&gt;repr&lt;/code&gt;, and the model gets metadata noise mixed into the context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To put agentic retrieval in the same position, wrap it in a &lt;code&gt;RunnableLambda&lt;/code&gt;, since it is a function rather than a retriever:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from langchain_core.runnables import RunnableLambda

def agentic_context(question: str) -&amp;gt; str:
    result = agentic_retrieve(
        knowledge_base_id=KB_ID,
        query=question,
        region_name=REGION,
        number_of_results=10,
    )
    return "\n\n".join(
        item.get("content", {}).get("text", "")
        for item in result.get("results", [])
    )

agentic_chain = (
    {"context": RunnableLambda(agentic_context), "question": RunnablePassthrough()}
    | prompt
    | llm
    | StrOutputParser()
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Note that &lt;code&gt;generate_response&lt;/code&gt; is off here. The service can generate the answer itself, but inside a chain you usually want your own prompt and model, so you take the chunks and generate downstream. Use the service generation when you want one call and less code, and the wrapped version when the prompt is yours to control.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="choosing-between-standard-and-agentic-retrieval"&gt;Choosing between standard and agentic retrieval&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use Retrieve for short, well-scoped questions. It is cheaper, faster, works against self-managed knowledge bases, and returns scores in the results. Most production traffic looks like this.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use AgenticRetrieveStream when questions are multi-part, comparative, or exploratory, or when the evidence spans more than one knowledge base. It registers up to five knowledge bases in one request and routes sub-queries using a natural-language description you attach to each. The other API cannot do this at all. It costs more per call, makes several model invocations, and has the higher latency of the two.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Routing on query shape rather than picking one for everything is the pattern we recommend. A classifier or a heuristic on the question can send most traffic down the cheap path and reserve the planner for questions that need it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Delete the knowledge base, its data source, the S3 objects and bucket, and the IAM role that you created. A knowledge base with documents in it continues to incur storage charges.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;bedrock_agent.delete_data_source(knowledgeBaseId=KB_ID, dataSourceId=DS_ID)
bedrock_agent.delete_knowledge_base(knowledgeBaseId=KB_ID)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-rag-bedrock-langchain-blog" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt; includes a cleanup script that also empties the bucket and removes the role.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We showed how to build a RAG application on Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases with LangChain, and how agentic retrieval handles multi-part questions that single-shot retrieval answers poorly. We also showed the friction in the current integration. Agentic retrieval is a function rather than a LangChain retriever, so it needs a &lt;code&gt;RunnableLambda&lt;/code&gt; to sit in a chain. The trace events that show the query plan require a direct &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt; call.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Agentic retrieval trades higher per-call cost for improved recall on multi-hop questions, using a built-in model for query planning. The next useful step is measuring your own query mix before you route everything through a planner.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, see the Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-build-managed.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the accompanying sample code. For help applying this to your own workload, contact your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Downgrading user roles in Amazon Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/downgrading-user-roles-in-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Gaurav Jaisingh]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 05 Oct 2026 15:51:38 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Sight]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2eb081af901222e14948443d7e3f56ca181d9a82</guid>

					<description>Amazon Quick doesn't offer a direct console path to downgrade a user from Admin or Author to Reader. This post walks through two reliable methods: a manual delete-and-recreate approach and an AWS CLI step-down sequence that downgrades roles safely while preserving asset ownership.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Managing access permissions effectively is an important aspect of maintaining a secure and collaborative environment in Amazon Quick. Quick supports versatile user management options designed to accommodate various identity types and organizational needs. You can provision users natively through Quick Identity or manage them through enterprise identity providers such as AWS IAM Identity Center or Active Directory. These systems allow user roles including Admin, Author, and Reader to be assigned and grouped according to job functions and security requirements. As team members join, change roles, or leave the organization, administrators must make sure transitions happen smoothly without disrupting business workflows or creating security gaps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Regular access reviews are important for maintaining security in your Quick environment. Plan monthly or quarterly audits of user roles to confirm everyone has appropriate permissions. When team members’ responsibilities change, proactively transfer ownership of their dashboards and analyses to prevent orphaned resources. This practice, recommended in the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/architecture/well-architected/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Framework&lt;/a&gt;, helps maintain continuity for business-critical visualizations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we focus on one specific but important user lifecycle task: downgrading user roles.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="why-downgrade-the-principle-of-least-privilege"&gt;Why downgrade?&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The principle of least privilege applies strongly to Quick administration. Users should have access only to what they need for their specific job functions. Downgrading user roles is a key part of enforcing least privilege. When a user’s responsibilities no longer require authoring or administrative capabilities, reduce their role accordingly to minimize the security surface area.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Quick pricing is also role-based: Authors and Admins pay a fixed monthly per-user fee, while Readers use session-based pricing. Organizations with users provisioned as Authors who only consume dashboards can reduce costs substantially by right-sizing them to Reader roles. For current pricing details, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksight/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick pricing page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For more granular control beyond the built-in roles in Amazon Quick, consider complementing role assignments with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/custom-permissions-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Custom Permissions&lt;/a&gt;, which restrict specific capabilities within a role tier. The integration of Amazon Quick with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; provides additional permission boundaries that complement the basic role system.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="scope-of-this-post"&gt;Scope of this post&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Although the exact steps depend on the user identity type, this post primarily addresses Amazon Quick Identity users (also called Quick-managed users). Users authenticated through IAM Identity Center or Active Directory typically have role changes managed through their external identity provider group mappings. If your environment uses IAM Identity Center, role downgrade is handled by moving the user from one IdC group to another (for example, from a Quick-Admins group to a Quick-Readers group). No step-down sequence is required.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Although the Amazon Quick console doesn’t provide a direct downgrade path for all role transitions (specifically, you cannot downgrade from Admin to Reader or from Author to Reader directly through the console interface), two reliable solutions exist: a manual deletion-and-recreation method, and an approach that uses the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). We walk you through both techniques to help you maintain proper access management as your team evolves.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before we begin, make sure you have an active AWS account with administrator access to Amazon Quick. If you plan to use the CLI method, you need the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CLI installed and configured&lt;/a&gt; on your machine. It’s also helpful to prepare a list of users whose roles need changing.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="understanding-amazon-quick-roles"&gt;Understanding Amazon Quick roles&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick offers two subscription tiers with distinct role sets:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Subscription&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Roles&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capabilities&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick Enterprise&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Admin Pro, Author Pro, Reader Pro&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Full BI + AI features (agents, topics, Q&amp;amp;A, stories, generative summaries)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/strong&gt; (BI-only)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Admin, Author, Reader&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Traditional BI authoring and consumption&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The console does not provide a direct way to downgrade from any Author tier to any Reader tier. The &lt;code&gt;update-user&lt;/code&gt; API enforces this same constraint, rejecting direct downgrades with a “You cannot downgrade a user role” error.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Amazon Quick Suite user management page, where the console offers no direct control to move a user from an Author tier down to a Reader tier. This illustrates why the methods in this post are necessary.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-1.png" alt="Amazon Quick Suite user management page showing users and their assigned roles" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Amazon Quick Suite user management page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The CLI step-down method works reliably for the &lt;strong&gt;legacy BI-only roles&lt;/strong&gt; (Admin, Author, Reader), following this sequence:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Admin &amp;gt; Author &amp;gt; Restricted Reader &amp;gt; Reader&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This same sequence also works for Pro users, as long as the intermediate steps use the legacy roles. For example, Author Pro &amp;gt; Author &amp;gt; Restricted Reader &amp;gt; Reader Pro completes successfully.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="important-considerations-before-making-changes"&gt;Important considerations before making changes&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When implementing role changes through either method, there are several important factors to keep in mind. First, verify that all users in your list are currently Admin or Author users before making changes. Attempting to downgrade users who already have lower permissions might cause errors. Resource ownership questions still apply even when using the CLI method. Users being downgraded will no longer be able to edit resources they previously owned. For larger organizations using the CLI method, consider loading user email addresses from a CSV file rather than hardcoding them. If you use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudshell/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudShell&lt;/a&gt; instead of a local CLI installation, you can omit the AWS Region specification because AWS CloudShell automatically uses your current console Region context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="transferring-asset-ownership-do-this-first"&gt;Transferring asset ownership (do this first)&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before deleting a user, it’s essential to make sure that any assets they own, such as dashboards, datasets, and analyses, are properly reassigned. This prevents disruptions and avoids leaving resources orphaned. If the user is an Author, verify whether they own any datasets or dashboards, and follow the same asset reassignment steps described here. There are three main ways to handle asset ownership transfers in Amazon Quick.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="option-1-proactively-transfer-ownership-to-another-admin"&gt;Option 1: Proactively transfer ownership to another admin&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The most controlled approach is to manually reassign ownership before deleting the user. To do this, go into each asset in Quick, choose &lt;strong&gt;Share&lt;/strong&gt;, and assign another admin as a co-owner. With this method, you can determine exactly who takes over each resource, which is especially useful for high-impact dashboards or datasets. Although this can be time-consuming in large environments, it gives you the flexibility to distribute assets according to your team’s structure and responsibilities.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the &lt;strong&gt;Share&lt;/strong&gt; dialog for an asset, where you add another admin as a co-owner so that ownership is transferred before the original user is removed.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick Suite Share dialog for adding a co-owner to an asset" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Transferring asset ownership to another user&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="option-2-use-the-amazon-quick-bulk-asset-transfer-on-the-admin-page"&gt;Option 2: Use the Amazon Quick bulk asset transfer on the Admin page&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If the user owns many assets, the manual method can become inefficient. In this case, you can use the &lt;strong&gt;Manage assets&lt;/strong&gt; feature available in the Admin section of Quick. With this tool, administrators can perform bulk ownership transfers or update sharing permissions for multiple assets at once. It streamlines the reassignment process significantly, particularly when offboarding users or managing organizational changes. For more details on how to use this feature, see the official &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/manage-qs-assets.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managing assets in Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; documentation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="option-3-share-assets-with-a-quick-user-group"&gt;Option 3: Share assets with a Quick user group&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Another effective strategy is to share assets with a user group. For Quick Identity users, you can create a Quick group, add relevant team members, and share dashboards or datasets with the group rather than individual users. If your account is integrated with IAM Identity Center or Active Directory, equivalent groups are created and managed in those systems, and Quick uses those external groups for access control instead of Quick-managed groups. This way, access to shared resources remains intact even if a specific user is deleted. It’s a resilient approach that reduces the need for reassigning ownership in the future and helps maintain consistent access across dynamic teams.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="manual-method-deleting-and-recreating-the-user"&gt;Manual method: Deleting and recreating the user&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Although not the most efficient approach, the manual deletion and recreation method is one option for environments where CLI usage isn’t feasible. This method involves removing the admin user entirely and then recreating them with reader permissions. This same manual method also applies when downgrading an Author to a Reader, though typically with fewer complications around asset ownership.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Because this method requires deleting the user account before recreating it with a lower role, careful preparation is essential to avoid losing valuable resources and disrupting workflows. Make sure you have completed the asset ownership transfer described in the previous section before proceeding.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-1-delete-the-admin-user"&gt;Step 1: Delete the admin user&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After you transfer ownership of all resources, sign in to the AWS Management Console and navigate to the Amazon Quick service. From there, choose your profile icon, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Manage Quick&lt;/strong&gt;, followed by &lt;strong&gt;Manage users&lt;/strong&gt;. When you locate the admin user you want to downgrade, choose the delete icon next to their name and confirm the deletion when prompted. This completely removes their current access to the system.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you haven’t transferred all resources beforehand, Quick presents an ownership transfer dialog. This dialog prompts you to select another admin who will receive ownership of all the user’s resources. Select an appropriate admin from the list, then confirm the transfer by choosing &lt;strong&gt;Delete and transfer&lt;/strong&gt;. This built-in transfer mechanism helps prevent orphaned resources but transfers everything to a single admin. For more granular control, use the proactive approach mentioned earlier to distribute resources strategically among different team members.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you skipped the proactive transfer, the deletion dialog shown in the following figure lets you reassign all of the user’s resources to a single admin before the account is removed.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/25/ML-22009-3.png" alt="Ownership transfer dialog prompting selection of an admin to receive the deleted user’s resources" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Built-in resource transfer during user deletion&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-2-recreate-the-user-with-the-reader-role"&gt;Step 2: Recreate the user with the Reader role&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After successfully deleting the user, remain on the Users page and choose &lt;strong&gt;Invite users&lt;/strong&gt;. Enter the user’s email address and select the &lt;strong&gt;Reader&lt;/strong&gt; role from the available options. Send the invitation to allow the user to rejoin Quick with their new, more restricted permissions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-3-verify-the-role-change"&gt;Step 3: Verify the role change&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After the user accepts the invitation:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that their permissions have been updated to &lt;strong&gt;Reader&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Verify that they can only view dashboards and reports.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that they can’t modify or create content.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cli-method-step-down-role-transition-recommended"&gt;CLI method: Step-down role transition (recommended)&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you prefer to use the AWS CLI or AWS CloudShell, you can programmatically change the user’s role. Because Quick requires role changes to be made in a specific sequence, you can’t transition directly from any Admin to any Reader. Instead, you must step through intermediate roles.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="important-notes"&gt;Important notes&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;your-account-id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with your AWS account ID.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;user-name&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with the username of the user.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;user-email&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with the user’s email address.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;If you’re using the AWS CLI outside CloudShell, specify the &lt;code&gt;--region&lt;/code&gt; parameter.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;--role&lt;/code&gt; value must match the API role name exactly (see the preceding table).&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="example-legacy-track-admin-to-reader"&gt;Example: Legacy track (Admin to Reader)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1:&lt;/strong&gt; Change role from Admin to Author:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight update-user \
  --aws-account-id &amp;lt;your-account-id&amp;gt; \
  --user-name &amp;lt;user-name&amp;gt; \
  --namespace default \
  --email &amp;lt;user-email&amp;gt; \
  --role AUTHOR&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2:&lt;/strong&gt; Update role from Author to Restricted Reader:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight update-user \
  --aws-account-id &amp;lt;your-account-id&amp;gt; \
  --user-name &amp;lt;user-name&amp;gt; \
  --namespace default \
  --email &amp;lt;user-email&amp;gt; \
  --role RESTRICTED_READER&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3:&lt;/strong&gt; Change role from Restricted Reader to Reader:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws quicksight update-user \
  --aws-account-id &amp;lt;your-account-id&amp;gt; \
  --user-name &amp;lt;user-name&amp;gt; \
  --namespace default \
  --email &amp;lt;user-email&amp;gt; \
  --role READER&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="after-the-downgrade-review-limit-profiles-and-custom-permissions"&gt;After the downgrade: Review Limit Profiles and Custom Permissions&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Role changes don’t automatically adjust Limit Profiles or Custom Permissions profiles. Both persist independently of the user’s role, and you should review them after any downgrade.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limit Profiles&lt;/strong&gt; control per-user caps on resources such as index storage and agent hours. If the downgraded user was assigned a profile appropriate for their previous Admin or Author role, reassign a Reader-appropriate limit profile to avoid over-allocating resources. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/limit-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Limit Profiles documentation&lt;/a&gt; for details.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom Permissions&lt;/strong&gt; restrict specific capabilities within a role tier. A profile assigned to an Author might not behave as expected on a Reader. Either unapply it during the final CLI step (using &lt;code&gt;--unapply-custom-permissions&lt;/code&gt;) or assign a profile designed for the Reader tier.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="script-for-updating-multiple-users"&gt;Script for updating multiple users&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use the following script to update multiple users.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;#!/bin/bash

# Define AWS account details
AWS_ACCOUNT_ID="&amp;lt;your-account-id&amp;gt;"
REGION="&amp;lt;your-region&amp;gt;"

# Load users from file (format: username,email per line)
# Lines starting with # are treated as comments
INPUT_FILE="users_to_downgrade.txt"

while IFS=',' read -r username email; do
  # Skip comments and empty lines
  [[ "$username" =~ ^#.*$ || -z "$username" ]] &amp;amp;&amp;amp; continue

  echo "Processing user: $username"

  # Role transition stages
  for ROLE in AUTHOR RESTRICTED_READER READER; do
    RESULT=$(aws quicksight update-user \
      --aws-account-id "$AWS_ACCOUNT_ID" \
      --user-name "$username" \
      --namespace default \
      --email "$email" \
      --role "$ROLE" \
      --region "$REGION" 2&amp;gt;&amp;amp;1)

    if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
      echo " ERROR at $ROLE: $RESULT"
      break
    fi

    echo " Transitioned to $ROLE"
    sleep 3
  done

  echo "Role update completed for $username."
done &amp;lt; "$INPUT_FILE"

echo "All user role updates processed!"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="script-functionality"&gt;Script functionality&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This script performs the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Reads usernames and email addresses from an external file (supports comments with #).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Updates role in three stages: Admin &amp;gt; Author &amp;gt; Restricted Reader &amp;gt; Reader.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Error handling stops the transition for a user if any step fails.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Sleep commands allow time for changes to take effect.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A few things to keep in mind when running the script:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm users are currently Admin or Author users before running.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use the actual Quick username, which might differ from the email prefix in federated environments.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;No Region specification needed in AWS CloudShell.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;You can modify the script to load users from a CSV export of &lt;code&gt;list-users&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-for-user-access-management"&gt;Best practices for user access management&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To keep your Quick environment secure and well-organized, follow these best practices:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Review user roles regularly (monthly or quarterly audits).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Transfer resource ownership before changing roles.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Follow the principle of least privilege.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use Custom Permissions profiles for fine-grained control within a role tier.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Share resources with groups rather than individuals for resilience.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use AWS Identity and Access Management for additional permission boundaries.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After completing user role changes:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Remove any test users.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Verify that no unintended resources remain.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Delete temporary CLI scripts or user list files.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Double-check the final user permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explored two methods to downgrade user roles in Amazon Quick: manual deletion and recreation, and a flexible AWS CLI approach. These strategies help you manage team access efficiently and securely.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For environments using IAM Identity Center, role changes are managed through IdC group reassignment, which does not require the step-down sequence described here. For additional governance controls, explore &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/custom-permissions-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Custom Permissions&lt;/a&gt; for feature-level restrictions and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/restricted-folders.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Restricted Folders&lt;/a&gt; for content-level isolation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/managing-user-access-qs-iam.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managing User Access in Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/manage-qs-assets.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managing Assets in Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/user/custom-permissions-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Custom Permissions Profiles&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/resources/best-practices/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Best Practices&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/business-intelligence/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Business Intelligence Blog&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We’d love to hear about your user management experiences. How does your organization handle role transitions in Quick Suite? Have you developed custom scripts or processes to streamline these changes? Share your thoughts and challenges in the comments.&lt;/p&gt;
