			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Build agent memory with NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit and Amazon S3 Vectors</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-agent-memory-with-nvidia-nemo-agent-toolkit-and-amazon-s3-vectors/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Venkata Sistla]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 01 Oct 2026 17:34:56 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2ecc9b560bbf992d1aef9266dfef2641bd2fd889</guid>

					<description>Learn how to use Amazon S3 Vectors as the persistent memory layer within the NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit (NAT), deployed on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS). This post shows how NAT's memory subsystem works and how to implement Amazon S3 Vectors as a custom memory provider, using a multi-agent investment research use case.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In my previous post: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-persistent-memory-for-multi-agent-ai-systems-with-amazon-s3-vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Building persistent memory for multi-agent AI systems with Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt;, we explored why memory engineering is the foundational discipline for production multi-agent systems. We showed how &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/s3-vectors.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), meets the architectural requirements for agent memory: semantic retrieval, rich metadata, strong consistency, and elastic scale.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we move from architecture to implementation. We show you how to use Amazon S3 Vectors as the persistent memory layer within the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit (NAT)&lt;/a&gt;, deployed on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt; for full operational control.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;By the end of this post, you will understand how NAT’s memory subsystem works and how to implement Amazon S3 Vectors as a custom memory provider. You will also learn how to deploy the stack on Amazon EKS, using a multi-agent investment research use case as the running example.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-is-nvidia-nemo-agent-toolkit"&gt;What is NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit?&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit (NAT)&lt;/a&gt; is an open source framework for building, profiling, and optimizing AI agents. It’s framework-agnostic, working with Strands Agents, LangChain, LlamaIndex, CrewAI, and custom implementations. NAT provides four capabilities relevant to production agent systems:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent orchestration&lt;/strong&gt; – Define agents as composable workflows with configurable large language models (LLMs), tools, and prompts. You run them locally with &lt;code&gt;nat run&lt;/code&gt; or as persistent services with &lt;code&gt;nat serve&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Profiling&lt;/strong&gt; – Track token usage, latency, throughput, and run times across agents and individual tools to identify bottlenecks in multi-agent workflows.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluation&lt;/strong&gt; – Built-in evaluators for answer accuracy, context relevance, response groundedness, and agent trajectory, with support for custom evaluators.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimization&lt;/strong&gt; – Automated hyperparameter tuning (temperature, &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;) that maximizes quality while minimizing cost and latency.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="nats-memory-module"&gt;NAT’s memory module&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;NAT includes a dedicated memory subsystem designed to store and retrieve conversation history, user preferences, and long-term knowledge across agent invocations. The memory module is extensible: you create custom memory providers (backends) by implementing NAT’s plugin interface. Key components include:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;MemoryEditor&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; – The abstract interface that all memory backends must implement. It defines three methods: &lt;code&gt;add_items()&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;search()&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;remove_items()&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;MemoryItem&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; – The data model representing a piece of memory, containing fields for conversation history, tags, metadata, &lt;code&gt;user_id&lt;/code&gt;, and an optional textual memory string.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;MemoryBaseConfig&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; – A Pydantic base class that custom memory configurations extend. NAT discovers providers via the &lt;code&gt;_type&lt;/code&gt; field in its YAML config.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automatic memory wrapper&lt;/strong&gt; – The &lt;code&gt;auto_memory_agent&lt;/code&gt; workflow type that wraps agents with automatic memory capture and retrieval, without requiring the LLM to explicitly invoke memory tools.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;NAT currently has these built-in memory providers: Mem0, MemMachine, Redis, and Zep. These cover common use cases. However, for production multi-agent systems that require elastic vector storage, strong write consistency, and cost-efficient scaling to billions of vectors, a custom provider backed by Amazon S3 Vectors is the right fit.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-s3-vectors-as-the-persistent-memory-backend"&gt;Why Amazon S3 Vectors as the persistent memory backend&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-persistent-memory-for-multi-agent-ai-systems-with-amazon-s3-vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Building persistent memory for multi-agent AI systems with Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt; covers the architectural rationale in depth. The following table summarizes the properties that make S3 Vectors the right fit for NAT’s memory layer:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requirement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;S3 Vectors capability&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Semantic retrieval&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Vector similarity search with configurable distance metrics (cosine, euclidean)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Scoped queries&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Filterable metadata on each vector (strings, numbers, Booleans, lists)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-agent coordination&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Strong write consistency. Memories are visible immediately after insertion&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Scale&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 2 billion vectors per index with no capacity planning&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Cost efficiency&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Pay only for storage, writes, and queries with no idle compute&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Access control&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies per bucket and index. Per-tenant indexes for hard isolation&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To follow along with the implementation in this post, you need:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/account/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with permissions to create Amazon S3 Vectors resources and Amazon EKS clusters.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An existing Amazon EKS cluster (see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/getting-started/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with Amazon EKS&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit&lt;/a&gt; installed (tested with version 1.6).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.11 or 3.12 (NAT requires &amp;gt;=3.11, &amp;lt;3.14).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An embedding model for generating vector representations (this post uses &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-embedding-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/install-kubectl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;kubectl&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/codecatalyst/latest/userguide/deploy-tut-eks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Docker&lt;/a&gt; for container and cluster operations.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="implementing-s3-vectors-as-a-nat-memory-provider"&gt;Implementing S3 Vectors as a NAT memory provider&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The implementation has three steps:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Step 1: Create the S3 Vectors infrastructure&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Step 2: Implement a custom MemoryEditor plugin&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Step 3: Configure the agent workflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 611px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/28/ML-21981-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/28/ML-21981-1.png" alt="Three-step flow: create the S3 Vectors infrastructure, implement a custom MemoryEditor plugin, and configure the agent workflow" width="601"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The three steps to implement Amazon S3 Vectors as a NAT memory provider&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-1.-create-the-amazon-s3-vectors-infrastructure"&gt;Step 1. Create the Amazon S3 Vectors infrastructure&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following code creates a vector bucket and an index with a metadata schema designed for agent memory. The index uses 1024 dimensions to match the output of Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2, and it marks the large content field as non-filterable metadata:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

REGION = "us-west-2"
VECTOR_BUCKET = "amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory"
INDEX_NAME = "agent-long-term-memory"

s3vectors = boto3.client("s3vectors", region_name=REGION)

# Create the vector bucket
s3vectors.create_vector_bucket(vectorBucketName=VECTOR_BUCKET)

# Create the index (1024 dimensions matches Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2)
s3vectors.create_index(
    vectorBucketName=VECTOR_BUCKET,
    indexName=INDEX_NAME,
    dataType="float32",
    dimension=1024,
    distanceMetric="cosine",
    metadataConfiguration={"nonFilterableMetadataKeys": ["content"]},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After creating the bucket and index, you can implement the memory provider that reads from and writes to them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-2.-implement-a-custom-memoryeditor-plugin"&gt;Step 2. Implement a custom MemoryEditor plugin&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following code implements the &lt;code&gt;MemoryEditor&lt;/code&gt; interface as an Amazon S3 Vectors backend and registers it so that NAT can discover it:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3
import json
import uuid
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from nat.plugin_api import MemoryBaseConfig, MemoryEditor, MemoryItem, register_memory
from nat.builder.builder import Builder


class S3VectorsMemoryConfig(MemoryBaseConfig, name="s3vectors_memory"):
    """NAT memory provider configuration for Amazon S3 Vectors."""
    vector_bucket: str
    index_name: str
    aws_region: str = "us-west-2"
    default_top_k: int = 5


class S3VectorsMemoryEditor(MemoryEditor):
    """NAT MemoryEditor backed by Amazon S3 Vectors."""

    def __init__(self, config: S3VectorsMemoryConfig):
        self._vector_bucket = config.vector_bucket
        self._index_name = config.index_name
        self._default_top_k = config.default_top_k
        self._s3vectors = boto3.client('s3vectors', region_name=config.aws_region)
        self._bedrock = boto3.client('bedrock-runtime', region_name=config.aws_region)

    def _get_embedding(self, text: str) -&amp;gt; list[float]:
        """Generate embeddings using Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2."""
        response = self._bedrock.invoke_model(
            modelId='amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0',
            contentType='application/json',
            accept='application/json',
            body=json.dumps({
                'inputText': text,
                'dimensions': 1024,
                'normalize': True
            })
        )
        return json.loads(response['body'].read())['embedding']

    async def add_items(self, items: list[MemoryItem], **kwargs) -&amp;gt; None:
        """Store memory items as vectors in Amazon S3 Vectors."""
        vectors = []
        for item in items:
            # Build the text to embed from the memory content
            text = item.memory or json.dumps(item.conversation)
            embedding = self._get_embedding(text)
            # Use uuid4 to prevent key collisions within the same second
            key = f"mem_{item.user_id}_{uuid.uuid4().hex[:12]}"
            # Note: S3 Vectors metadata values have size limits.
            # Truncate content to 1024 characters for production use.
            content_for_metadata = text[:1024]
            mem_metadata = {
                'user_id': item.user_id,
                'memory_type': item.metadata.get('memory_type', 'episodic'),
                'agent_id': item.metadata.get('agent_id', ''),
                'team_id': item.metadata.get('team_id', ''),
                'task_id': item.metadata.get('task_id', ''),
                'confidence': item.metadata.get('confidence', 0.8),
                'created_at_epoch': int(datetime.now(timezone.utc).timestamp()),
                'is_shared': item.metadata.get('is_shared', True),
                'source': item.metadata.get('source', 'agent'),
                'content': content_for_metadata,
            }
            # Add domain-specific metadata if present
            if 'ticker' in item.metadata:
                mem_metadata['ticker'] = item.metadata['ticker']
            vectors.append({
                'key': key,
                'data': {'float32': embedding},
                'metadata': mem_metadata
            })
        self._s3vectors.put_vectors(
            vectorBucketName=self._vector_bucket,
            indexName=self._index_name,
            vectors=vectors
        )

    async def search(self, query: str, top_k: int = None, **kwargs) -&amp;gt; list[MemoryItem]:
        """Retrieve semantically relevant memories from Amazon S3 Vectors."""
        query_embedding = self._get_embedding(query)
        effective_top_k = top_k or self._default_top_k
        # Build metadata filter from kwargs
        filter_expr = {}
        for field in ('agent_id', 'memory_type', 'ticker', 'team_id', 'user_id'):
            if field in kwargs:
                filter_expr[field] = {'$eq': kwargs[field]}
        # Support boolean filter for is_shared
        if 'is_shared' in kwargs:
            filter_expr['is_shared'] = {'$eq': kwargs['is_shared']}
        response = self._s3vectors.query_vectors(
            vectorBucketName=self._vector_bucket,
            indexName=self._index_name,
            queryVector={'float32': query_embedding},
            topK=effective_top_k,
            filter=filter_expr if filter_expr else None,
            returnMetadata=True
        )
        results = []
        for vec in response.get('vectors', []):
            results.append(MemoryItem(
                conversation=[],
                tags=[vec['metadata'].get('memory_type', '')],
                metadata=vec['metadata'],
                user_id=vec['metadata'].get('user_id', ''),
                memory=vec['metadata'].get('content', '')
            ))
        return results

    async def remove_items(self, **kwargs) -&amp;gt; None:
        """Remove memory items from Amazon S3 Vectors."""
        keys = kwargs.get('keys', [])
        if keys:
            self._s3vectors.delete_vectors(
                vectorBucketName=self._vector_bucket,
                indexName=self._index_name,
                keys=keys
            )


# Register the memory provider so NAT can discover it
@register_memory(config_type=S3VectorsMemoryConfig)
async def build_s3vectors_memory(config: S3VectorsMemoryConfig, builder: Builder):
    yield S3VectorsMemoryEditor(config)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The plugin generates embeddings with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2, stores each memory as a vector with scoped metadata, and translates search filters into Amazon S3 Vectors metadata queries.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-3.-configure-the-nat-agent-workflow"&gt;Step 3. Configure the NAT agent workflow&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the plugin defined, configure it in NAT’s YAML config and wire it into an agent workflow:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;# config.yml - NAT agent configuration with Amazon S3 Vectors memory
memory:
  agent_memory:
    _type: s3vectors_memory
    vector_bucket: "amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory"
    index_name: "agent-long-term-memory"
    aws_region: "us-west-2"

functions:
  add_memory:
    _type: add_memory
    memory: agent_memory
    description: |
      Store important findings, patterns, or facts to long-term memory.
      Use this after discovering new information during research.
  get_memory:
    _type: get_memory
    memory: agent_memory
    description: |
      Retrieve relevant prior knowledge before starting research.
      Query with the current research topic to recall related findings.
  web_search:
    _type: web_search
  financial_data:
    _type: financial_data_api

workflow:
  _type: auto_memory_agent
  inner_agent_name: research_agent
  memory_name: agent_memory
  llm_name: bedrock_llm
  save_user_messages_to_memory: true
  retrieve_memory_for_every_response: true
  save_ai_messages_to_memory: true

llm:
  bedrock_llm:
    _type: bedrock
    model_id: "anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-20250514"
    temperature: 0.3&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the &lt;code&gt;auto_memory_agent&lt;/code&gt; wrapper, you can capture and retrieve memory automatically. User messages and agent responses are stored, and relevant context is injected before each agent call. This design removes the need for the LLM to explicitly invoke memory tools.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="responsible-ai-and-data-handling-considerations"&gt;Responsible AI and data handling considerations&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Because this solution persists conversation history and user memory, plan for how that data is retained and accessed. Set a retention policy for stored memories, and use the memory consolidation and removal paths in this post to expire data you no longer need. Avoid storing personally identifiable information (PII) in memory metadata, and redact or tokenize sensitive fields before you embed them. Scope access with per-tenant indexes and least-privilege IAM policies so that each agent reads and writes only the memory it owns.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="putting-amazon-s3-vectors-in-practice-multi-agent-investment-research"&gt;Putting Amazon S3 Vectors in practice: Multi-agent investment research&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following use case demonstrates this pattern in practice. Consider three specialized agents collaborating on an investment research tool:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Research Agent&lt;/strong&gt; – Gathers market data, earnings reports, and news.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Analysis Agent&lt;/strong&gt; – Performs quantitative analysis and identifies patterns.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Synthesis Agent&lt;/strong&gt; – Produces reports combining findings from both agents.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With persistent memory, each agent builds on prior work. The Research Agent recalls previously collected data, avoiding redundant API calls. The Analysis Agent builds on patterns identified in prior sessions. The Synthesis Agent accesses cumulative findings to produce progressively richer reports.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="multi-agent-memory-coordination"&gt;Multi-agent memory coordination&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each agent uses the same S3 Vectors index but writes with its own &lt;code&gt;agent_id&lt;/code&gt; in the metadata. Agents retrieve shared knowledge using metadata filters:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Analysis Agent retrieves Research Agent's shared findings
research_findings = await memory_client.search(
    query=f"Recent research findings for {ticker}",
    top_k=10,
    team_id='investment-research',
    is_shared=True
)

# Synthesis Agent queries all team knowledge
all_team_knowledge = await memory_client.search(
    query=f"Complete analysis and research for {ticker} Q2 2026",
    top_k=20,
    team_id='investment-research'
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;NAT’s built-in multi-tenant memory isolation uses &lt;code&gt;user_id&lt;/code&gt; to scope memory per user. For multi-agent team coordination, the &lt;code&gt;team_id&lt;/code&gt; metadata field provides an additional grouping dimension within the same S3 Vectors index.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="memory-consolidation"&gt;Memory consolidation&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Over time, episodic memories accumulate. Periodically consolidating them into semantic memories (generalized knowledge) keeps retrieval precise and efficient. You can trigger consolidation through a scheduled cron job, a vector count threshold, or an agent-initiated signal after a configurable number of research cycles:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;async def consolidate_memories(ticker: str, memory_client, workflow):
    """Distill episodic memories into durable semantic knowledge."""
    episodes = await memory_client.search(
        query=f"All findings about {ticker}",
        top_k=50,
        memory_type='episodic',
        ticker=ticker
    )
    episode_texts = [e.memory for e in episodes if e.memory]
    consolidation_prompt = f"""Given these {len(episode_texts)} observations
about {ticker}, identify durable patterns and generalized knowledge.

{chr(10).join(episode_texts)}

Return a JSON array of insight strings. Do not include specific dates
or one-time events."""
    response = await workflow.run(consolidation_prompt)
    # Parse the response into a list of insights
    insights = json.loads(response)
    # Store each consolidated insight as semantic memory
    await memory_client.add_items([
        MemoryItem(
            conversation=[],
            tags=['semantic'],
            metadata={
                'memory_type': 'semantic',
                'confidence': 0.9,
                'ticker': ticker,
                'source': 'consolidation',
                'is_shared': True,
                'team_id': 'investment-research'
            },
            user_id='system',
            memory=insight
        )
        for insight in insights
    ])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="deploying-agent-workloads-on-amazon-eks"&gt;Deploying agent workloads on Amazon EKS&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon EKS is a deliberate choice for teams that need full operational control over their agent workloads. With Amazon EKS, you control scaling, networking, and lifecycle management, and you integrate natively with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) for Amazon S3 Vectors access. If you prefer a fully managed runtime, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore provides a managed alternative that removes this operational overhead. You run NAT agents as containerized services using &lt;code&gt;nat serve&lt;/code&gt;. Each agent type is a Kubernetes Deployment with IAM access granted through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/iam-roles-for-service-accounts.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA).&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-1-build-the-agent-container"&gt;Step 1: Build the agent container&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following Dockerfile packages the agent with its configuration and memory plugin:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-dockerfile"&gt;FROM python:3.12-slim
RUN pip install nvidia-nat[langchain] boto3
COPY config.yml /app/config.yml
COPY s3vectors_memory.py /app/s3vectors_memory.py
WORKDIR /app
CMD ["nat", "serve", "--config_file", "config.yml"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-2-deploy-with-kubernetes-manifests"&gt;Step 2: Deploy with Kubernetes manifests&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following manifests deploy the Research Agent with autoscaling:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: research-agent
  namespace: agent-team
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: research-agent
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: research-agent
        team: investment-research
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: agent-sa
      containers:
      - name: agent
        image: ${ECR_REGISTRY}/research-agent:latest # Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
        env:
        - name: VECTOR_BUCKET
          value: "amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory"
        - name: INDEX_NAME
          value: "agent-long-term-memory"
        - name: AWS_REGION
          value: "us-west-2"
        resources:
          requests:
            cpu: "500m"
            memory: "1Gi"
          limits:
            cpu: "2000m"
            memory: "4Gi"
---
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: research-agent-hpa
  namespace: agent-team
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: research-agent
  minReplicas: 1
  maxReplicas: 10
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 70&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="iam-policy-for-s3-vectors-access"&gt;IAM policy for S3 Vectors access&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following IAM policy grants scoped access to the memory layer. Attach it to the IAM role associated with the &lt;code&gt;agent-sa&lt;/code&gt; service account through IRSA:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "s3vectors:PutVectors",
                "s3vectors:QueryVectors",
                "s3vectors:GetVectors",
                "s3vectors:DeleteVectors"
            ],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:s3vectors:us-west-2:${ACCOUNT_ID}:vector-bucket/amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory/*"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-and-verify"&gt;Deploy and verify&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After building and pushing the container image, deploy the agents:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Create the namespace
kubectl create namespace agent-team

# Apply the manifests
kubectl apply -f kubernetes/research-agent-deployment.yaml

# Verify pods are running
kubectl get pods -n agent-team

# Test the agent endpoint
kubectl port-forward -n agent-team svc/research-agent 8000:8000
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/generate \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"inputs": "What are the latest AAPL earnings trends?"}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;S3 Vectors provides strong write consistency. Pods and agent types can immediately see memories that agent pods store, with no cache invalidation required. The &lt;code&gt;HorizontalPodAutoscaler&lt;/code&gt; scales agent replicas based on CPU utilization. Each replica connects to the same S3 Vectors index through IRSA, supporting consistent memory access regardless of which pod handles a given request.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="evaluating-memory-impact"&gt;Evaluating memory impact&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With NAT’s evaluation harness (&lt;code&gt;nat eval&lt;/code&gt;), you can quantify the effect of memory on agent performance. Configure two evaluation runs: one with memory enabled and one without, using the same dataset:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Evaluate with memory enabled
nat eval --config_file config_with_memory.yml \
  --dataset eval_dataset.jsonl \
  --metrics accuracy,groundedness,token_usage,latency

# Evaluate without memory (baseline)
nat eval --config_file config_no_memory.yml \
  --dataset eval_dataset.jsonl \
  --metrics accuracy,groundedness,token_usage,latency&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Based on the architectural properties of this design, you can expect the following qualitative outcomes when comparing these runs. These are directional expectations, not benchmarked measurements:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Groundedness improves&lt;/strong&gt; – Recalled memories provide verifiable source context that the agent can cite rather than relying on parametric knowledge alone.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token usage decreases&lt;/strong&gt; – Agents skip re-deriving facts already established in prior sessions. The reduction scales with how much repeated context your workflows involve.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency increases modestly&lt;/strong&gt; – Each memory recall adds an S3 Vectors query (subsecond at standard access patterns), typically a small fraction of overall LLM inference time.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Duplicate work drops&lt;/strong&gt; – With shared memory, agents avoid repeating research or analysis that teammates have already completed.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The magnitude of these effects depends on your workload: how much context is reused across sessions, how many agents coordinate, and how large your retrieval budget (top-k) is. NAT’s hyperparameter optimizer can systematically tune &lt;code&gt;top_k&lt;/code&gt; and similarity threshold to find the optimal cost-quality trade-off for your use case.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges from resources created in this walkthrough, delete the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon EKS Deployment and associated resources:
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl delete namespace agent-team&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the S3 Vectors index and vector bucket:
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;s3vectors.delete_index(vectorBucketName='amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory', indexName='agent-long-term-memory')
s3vectors.delete_vector_bucket(vectorBucketName='amzn-s3-demo-research-agent-memory')&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the IAM role and policy created for IRSA if no longer needed.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post showed how to implement a custom NAT memory provider backed by Amazon S3 Vectors and deploy it on Amazon EKS. The key building blocks:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NAT’s &lt;code&gt;MemoryEditor&lt;/code&gt; interface&lt;/strong&gt; provides the plugin contract for custom memory backends.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;auto_memory_agent&lt;/code&gt; wrapper handles capture and retrieval automatically.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/strong&gt; delivers the durable, semantically queryable backend with strong consistency for multi-agent coordination.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EKS&lt;/strong&gt; gives you full operational control over agent lifecycle, scaling, and network isolation.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;These patterns apply to domains where agents benefit from accumulated knowledge. They include classifying memory as episodic, semantic, or procedural, sharing memory across agents with metadata filtering, and consolidating memory with an LLM. Examples include customer support, DevOps automation, legal research, and scientific discovery.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For the memory architecture foundations referenced throughout this post, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-persistent-memory-for-multi-agent-ai-systems-with-amazon-s3-vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Building persistent memory for multi-agent AI systems with Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with NAT, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Agent Toolkit documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/agent-toolkit/latest/extend/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Adding a Memory Provider guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<title>Uplifting conversion across the acquisition funnel with personalization using contextual bandits on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/uplifting-conversion-across-the-acquisition-funnel-with-personalization-using-contextual-bandits-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Chidi Prince John]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 01 Oct 2026 16:51:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">6c1b27922d7a01ad3cade26f5450a719211cf6d1</guid>

					<description>Generative AI makes it cheap to produce personalized content at scale, but which variation do you show each customer? Amazon Payments used a multi-objective contextual bandit on Amazon SageMaker AI to personalize an acquisition funnel, achieving a high single-digit conversion lift for one audience, and learning why content, not the model, was the constraint.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Generative AI has made it possible to produce large amounts of personalized content quickly and at low cost. In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reinvent-personalization-with-generative-ai-on-amazon-bedrock-using-task-decomposition-for-agentic-workflows/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;our previous post&lt;/a&gt;, we showed how generative AI on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; can produce personalized content at scale while staying within brand guidelines and guardrails. The new challenge is now one of selection. Among all of those options, which one do you show each customer, and how long does it take to learn the answer? This post tackles the selection challenge that follows the proliferation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we share how Amazon Payments applied AI-based personalization to a product acquisition funnel, using a multi-objective contextual multi-armed bandit (MAB) on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;. In a seven-week online A/B test we currently see a high single-digit percentage relative lift in final-funnel conversion for one customer population, while another saw no improvement over the existing experience. The problem turned out to be the content, not the model. We cover the intuition behind bandits, our extension to optimize an entire conversion funnel, and the AWS architecture behind the solution. We also share &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-objective-contextual-bandit-with-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;a code repository&lt;/a&gt; that you can use to test this approach on synthetic data and understand the method hands-on using Amazon SageMaker AI.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="growing-role-of-multi-armed-bandits-in-the-generative-ai-era"&gt;Growing role of multi-armed bandits in the generative AI era&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-armed_bandit" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A multi-armed bandit (MAB)&lt;/a&gt; is a reinforcement learning method built for settings with many options and limited traffic. It learns which option performs best while continuing to serve customers. It treats each content variation as an “arm,” tries each against live traffic, and steadily shifts impressions toward the arms that perform, while holding a fraction back to keep testing the rest. This is the fundamental trade-off between exploitation (serve the current best arm) and exploration (try less-certain arms to gather evidence). Because a bandit never stops doing both, it keeps improving as new variations are added, and it never has to wait for a test to conclude. A/B/n testing still has its place, but as generative AI accelerates the number of variations to learn from, we expect bandits to play a growing role.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;There are several bandit selection strategies in the literature: epsilon-greedy, Upper Confidence Bound (UCB), Thompson sampling, among others. For a deeper introduction to these methods and their deployment on AWS, see our earlier post &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/dynamic-a-b-testing-for-machine-learning-models-with-amazon-sagemaker-mlops-projects/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamic A/B testing for machine learning models with Amazon SageMaker MLOps Projects&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In Amazon Payments, we chose UCB, a strategy that selects the arm with the highest estimated reward plus an uncertainty bonus, naturally balancing exploitation and exploration. Its deterministic selection rule gives an auditable, reproducible decision for every impression, which makes sure every serving decision can be explained and reproduced if needed. However, a standard bandit learns one best arm for the entire audience. Personalization requires conditioning on who the visitor is, and that is what contextual bandits provide.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="adding-the-context-using-contextual-bandits"&gt;Adding the context using contextual bandits&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Instead of asking “which content is the best overall?,” a contextual bandit conditions its decision on signals about the visit. Segmented bandits run a separate instance per hand-defined group, but the groups are arbitrary and each needs its own traffic. Contextual bandits condition directly on a feature vector (a numeric representation of attributes), so a pattern learned in one context transfers to every similar visit without separate per-group traffic. Our production system represents each customer as a context vector of behavioral signals (payment behavior, transaction mix, and similar features) in place of a fixed segment. The entity ID (an opaque key such as &lt;code&gt;entity_id&lt;/code&gt;) is used only to route the learned recommendation back to the right visitor. It is never a model input.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="linucb-learning-at-the-feature-level"&gt;LinUCB: Learning at the feature level&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For a contextual approach, we selected Linear UCB (LinUCB), introduced by &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1003.0146" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Li et al.&amp;nbsp;(2010)&lt;/a&gt;. While newer bandit algorithms exist, LinUCB remains a battle-tested method that is computationally efficient, auditable (deterministic arm selection), and naturally handles a large arm space with limited warm-start data. Its key assumption is that the expected reward for an arm is a linear function of the context vector, which lets it generalize to visitors it has not seen before.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each arm keeps two running tallies updated on every impression:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;b&lt;/code&gt;, the reward ledger:&lt;/strong&gt; which visitor signals led to conversions (b += reward · x).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;A&lt;/code&gt;, the experience ledger:&lt;/strong&gt; which visitors the arm has seen (A += x·xᵀ, starting from identity).&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Dividing reward by experience gives the estimate (θ = A⁻¹·b). The experience ledger also shrinks the exploration bonus as evidence grows. The arm’s score is:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;score(a, x) = θᵀ·x + α · √(xᵀ · A⁻¹ · x)
              \_____/   \________________/
               estimate   uncertainty bonus&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The bonus is context-dependent. It’s large for a kind of visitor the arm has rarely seen, and small for one it has seen often. A plain bandit explores at a single global rate, while LinUCB adjusts how much it explores for every visitor it scores.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="optimizing-an-entire-funnel"&gt;Optimizing an entire funnel&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The customer journey in our use case comprises three steps: application start, submission, and approval. The objective is therefore a multi-stage outcome, not a single metric. These stages do not move in unison. Content optimized for starts tends to attract a broad audience, yet approval depends on whether the offer genuinely suits the applicant. Optimizing one stage in isolation can degrade another. We refer to this as the seesaw problem. Conversely, optimizing solely for approvals starves the model of signal, because approvals are rare and delayed.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Our approach optimizes the entire funnel simultaneously by running one LinUCB model per stage (start, submit, approve) and combining their UCB scores through a linear combination:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;selected_arm = argmax_a [ w_start   · UCB_start(a, x)
                        + w_submit  · UCB_submit(a, x)
                        + w_approve · UCB_approve(a, x) ]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The stage weights can be assigned based on business priorities or learned by a separate calibration step. We used approximately equal weights. In practice, one could weight approvals more heavily once the model is warmed or use a Pareto frontier if the trade-off is genuinely contested.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Here’s the core of the model in Python. Each LinUCBDisjoint maintains independent parameters per arm, and MultiObjectiveLinUCB composes three of them:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import numpy as np


class LinUCBDisjoint:
    """LinUCB with disjoint (independent) parameters per arm.
    Each arm maintains its own reward and experience matrices."""

    def __init__(self, n_arms, n_features, alpha=1.0):
        self.n_arms = n_arms
        self.n_features = n_features
        self.alpha = alpha  # exploration-exploitation trade-off parameter
        # A: experience matrix (d x d); b: reward vector (d) - one per arm
        self.A = [np.eye(n_features) for _ in range(n_arms)]
        self.b = [np.zeros(n_features) for _ in range(n_arms)]

    def update(self, arm, context, reward):
        """Exact, incremental update - no retraining needed."""
        self.A[arm] += np.outer(context, context)
        self.b[arm] += reward * context


class MultiObjectiveLinUCB:
    """Combines one LinUCB per funnel stage via weighted sum."""

    def __init__(self, n_arms, n_features, alpha=1.0,
                 weights=(1/3, 1/3, 1/3)):  # stage weights (start, submit, approve)
        self.n_arms = n_arms
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.weights = weights  # [start, submit, approve]
        self.app_start_bandit = LinUCBDisjoint(n_arms, n_features, alpha)
        self.app_submit_bandit = LinUCBDisjoint(n_arms, n_features, alpha)
        self.app_approved_bandit = LinUCBDisjoint(n_arms, n_features, alpha)

    def select_arm(self, context):
        combined = []
        for a in range(self.n_arms):
            score = 0.0
            for w, bandit in zip(
                self.weights,
                [self.app_start_bandit,
                 self.app_submit_bandit,
                 self.app_approved_bandit],
            ):
                A_inv = np.linalg.inv(bandit.A[a])
                theta = A_inv @ bandit.b[a]
                ucb = theta @ context + self.alpha * np.sqrt(
                    context @ A_inv @ context)
                score += w * ucb
            combined.append(score)
        return int(np.argmax(combined))

    def update(self, arm, context, r_start, r_submit, r_approve):
        self.app_start_bandit.update(arm, context, r_start)
        self.app_submit_bandit.update(arm, context, r_submit)
        self.app_approved_bandit.update(arm, context, r_approve)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We built &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-objective-contextual-bandit-with-sagemaker/blob/main/notebooks/walkthrough.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;a quick start Jupyter notebook&lt;/a&gt; to test this approach on Amazon SageMaker AI. &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-objective-contextual-bandit-with-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;The repository&lt;/a&gt; also includes a command-line demo and unit tests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tuning the alpha.&lt;/strong&gt; The α parameter controls the exploration-exploitation balance. Higher values encourage exploration of under-tested arms, and lower values favor exploitation. A value of α = 1.0 is a reasonable default (Li et al., 2010). In practice, start higher when the arm space is large and history is limited, then reduce α as evidence accumulates. A typical range is 0.1 to 2.0. Values that are too high waste traffic on weak arms, and values that are too low risk locking onto a suboptimal arm. Because α is a single scalar, a grid search is straightforward.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Handling delayed feedback.&lt;/strong&gt; In our setting, approval decisions lag by days, so the reward for the final funnel stage isn’t observed until well after the impression. We address this with an attribution window. Starts and submissions update the model immediately, while approval outcomes are held back until the subsequent batch cycle, avoiding downward bias from pending applications. This aligns naturally with our weekly processing cadence.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="publishing-safe-content-at-scale"&gt;Publishing safe content at scale&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A bandit is only as good as the pool of arms. Building a rich arm pool responsibly is the next problem to solve at scale while maintaining content oversight.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The key idea is to compose many variations from a small set of reviewed building blocks. Our content was assembled from two kinds of parts: &lt;em&gt;industry-themed images&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;benefit-focused taglines&lt;/em&gt;. The arm space is the Cartesian product of these parts, so each arm is one (image, tagline) pairing and a modest number of building blocks produces a large pool of distinct page variations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-20979-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-20979-1.png" alt="Vetted building blocks (industry-themed images and benefit-focused taglines) combined into the full arm pool" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Building blocks are vetted individually, then combined into the full arm pool&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That structure also makes the approach scalable without sacrificing oversight, through a few layers of control.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Vetting the parts, not the combinations.&lt;/strong&gt; Some content elements are fixed by stakeholder requirements. Rather than reviewing every possible combination, which would be impractical as the space grows, we vet each individual building block up front. Because the number of building blocks remains small and reviewable while the combinations grow rapidly, vetting the parts once provides assurance over the entire combinatorial space.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Design systems for consistency.&lt;/strong&gt; As described in our earlier post, anchoring content to a design system (approved colors, layouts, components, and patterns) keeps variations visually consistent and on-brand by construction, not by chance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expanding the pool with generative AI.&lt;/strong&gt; In our initial deployment, building blocks were curated with detailed human oversight, even when production was assisted by generative AI tools. We see clear potential to increase the parts pool (text, imagery, layouts) substantially, which would widen the bandit’s selection space. As this pool grows, the bandit and the generative pipeline form a virtuous cycle. Generative AI widens the candidate set, and the bandit identifies which combinations yield the strongest outcomes per visitor.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="deploying-on-aws"&gt;Deploying on AWS&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI is an AI model development service that supports the full model lifecycle. It’s the primary AWS service behind our end-to-end pipeline, providing model training, batch processing, version control, and monitoring in a unified tool. We adopted a batch architecture for two reasons: the offline nature of the selection problem (feedback accumulates over days), and the observation that visitor selection behavior does not shift rapidly enough to require real-time model updates. A scheduled SageMaker AI Processing job reads the prior period’s feedback, updates the model, and writes fresh recommendations for the next period.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We run a weekly SageMaker AI batch job that reads customer outcomes from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;, updates the bandit model, and publishes per-customer recommendations to a low-latency key-value store. Starting with a weekly cadence provides a conservative baseline. Increasing frequency is straightforward once the model demonstrates stable lift. Approval feedback, which can lag by days, is incorporated into subsequent batches.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-20979-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/29/ML-20979-2.png" alt="End-to-end batch bandit architecture with Amazon S3 model state and low-latency key-value serving" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end batch bandit architecture with Amazon S3 model state and key-value serving&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Data collection.&lt;/strong&gt; Customer impressions and outcomes (starts, submissions, approvals) are logged at end-of-session and flow into Amazon S3 as observations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Model update and inference&lt;/strong&gt;. An Amazon SageMaker AI Processing job runs the Python entry point. It loads the most recent model state from Amazon S3, discovering the latest dated model prefix automatically so that nothing needs reconfiguring between runs. It then splits the data into feedback and inference rows, updates the model on the feedback (incremental updates), and scores every prospect to select an arm. We use a Processing job because our workload is a single, self-contained step that both updates and scores, with custom S3 I/O. Neither a Training job nor Batch Transform maps cleanly to this pattern.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Persistence and output.&lt;/strong&gt; The job writes the updated model state back to Amazon S3 (in a new dated path, which gives a built-in version history and straightforward rollback), alongside the observations and the arm content. The per-customer recommendations (each customer mapped to their selected arm) are published to a low-latency key-value store (such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;) that fronts live traffic.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Warm-starting the model.&lt;/strong&gt; We warm-started each model from a period of randomized content assignment, which provides unbiased data that lets the bandit initialize its beliefs without selection bias and spend its exploration budget more efficiently from day one.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scaling inference efficiently.&lt;/strong&gt; Scoring a large prospect population is compute-intensive. Two optimizations kept it within budget: precomputing the arm matrix inversions once (they do not change within a batch), and splitting the prospect set into chunks scored in parallel using Python’s &lt;code&gt;multiprocessing.Pool&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How the page is served.&lt;/strong&gt; The serving path is straightforward because the heavy lifting has already happened in the batch job. The personalized page is delivered by a low-latency key-value store (such as Amazon DynamoDB) that holds each customer’s precomputed recommendation. When a customer arrives, the page performs a single lookup by entity ID and renders the precomputed arm’s content. There is no real-time model inference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For use cases that require real-time scoring, where context is only known at request time or content must adapt within a session, Amazon SageMaker AI real-time inference endpoints are the natural alternative, hosting the model behind a low-latency API.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The design has one more robustness property worth calling out. If no recommendation exists for a visitor, the page falls back to the default static experience, so no customer is worse off than baseline. This fallback bounds the downside and makes incremental rollout safer.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned"&gt;Lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deploying this system in production taught us several things that generalize beyond our specific use case.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="model-design"&gt;Model design&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;When populations differ a lot, train separately.&lt;/em&gt; If your audience splits into groups with significantly different features or conversion rates, a single shared model lets the larger or noisier group dominate what gets learned. Training one model per group, giving each its own feature set and arm space, lets each audience’s signal come through cleanly.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Optimize the funnel, not a proxy.&lt;/em&gt; The single most important design decision was going multi-objective. A single-stage bandit would have let us “win” on an upstream metric while degrading performance on the downstream metric that ultimately determined business value. We validated this empirically before deployment. Policies optimized for a single funnel stage consistently produced at least one negative directional lift elsewhere. The multi-objective formulation was the only approach that maintained non-negative estimates across all three stages simultaneously. If your business goal is several steps removed from the first click, encode all the steps in the reward.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="content-strategy"&gt;Content strategy&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The selection algorithm addresses only half of the challenge. The quality of the content pool is equally important. In our A/B test, one customer population saw directionally positive lifts across all three funnel metrics, currently a high single-digit percentage relative lift on the final stage. For the second population, the model explored most of its arm pool yet found no combination that beat the static page. The lifts were negative, and the approval regression was statistically significant. This was not a model failure. Thorough exploration can confirm that a content pool contains no winner.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If a bandit explores the arms broadly and still cannot beat the control (the static experience, with no bandit), then the arms need revisiting instead of the algorithm. This is where generative content pipelines, like the one in our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reinvent-personalization-with-generative-ai-on-amazon-bedrock-using-task-decomposition-for-agentic-workflows/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;earlier post&lt;/a&gt;, become essential. They expand and refresh the pool the bandit draws from, so the model has a better chance of finding a winning combination.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Consider making the baseline an arm. Including the existing default experience as one of the arms means that if no personalized arm beats the default for a given customer, UCB naturally gravitates toward serving it, bounding how far the system can regress.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="evaluation"&gt;Evaluation&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Offline evaluation and A/B testing answer different questions. Offline replay (policy compared to random on held-out data) tells you “does the model beat random assignment?”, serving as an initial validation. But only a conventional A/B test (bandit-personalized compared to static baseline) determines whether personalization outperforms your existing experience. We used both. Offline estimates gave confidence to launch, and the A/B test delivered the definitive verdict, including the finding that content pool quality was the binding constraint on one population.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The weights tell you what is driving the outcome. LinUCB’s learned θ vectors are directly interpretable. A large positive weight on a feature means customers strong on that signal are more likely to convert on that arm. After a few weeks you can ask “what signals differentiated responders to this image?” and get credible insights. This yields built-in interpretability without additional tooling, and provides a direct input to content strategy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="operations"&gt;Operations&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Batch is a legitimate deployment pattern. You do not need a real-time online-learning application to run a bandit in production. A weekly Processing job reading feedback, updating an exact-update model, and writing recommendations to S3 was auditable, resource-efficient, and a natural fit for delayed feedback.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusions"&gt;Conclusions&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Generative AI removed the constraint on producing personalized content. The remaining challenge is deciding which content to show whom and learning the answer from real customer feedback. Multi-armed bandits are purpose-built for this. With a contextual, multi-objective formulation, you can personalize on individual behavior while optimizing an entire conversion funnel.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The system is straightforward to operate on AWS. A scheduled SageMaker AI Processing job plus Amazon S3 for versioned model state gives you incremental learning, an audit trail, and a safe fallback without requiring a specialized real-time tool.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If generative AI is already filling your content pool, a contextual bandit is the natural optimization layer on top, identifying for each individual which content variant yields the strongest outcome. The two techniques in this post series are complementary.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI console&lt;/a&gt;, explore the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI developer guide&lt;/a&gt;, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-objective-contextual-bandit-with-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;code sample&lt;/a&gt; and open its walkthrough notebook to run the bandit yourself, and review our earlier post on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reinvent-personalization-with-generative-ai-on-amazon-bedrock-using-task-decomposition-for-agentic-workflows/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generative AI personalization on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
